Testing Thresholds and Spectral Properties of High-Dimensional Random Toroidal Graphs via Edgeworth-Style Expansions.
Samuel Baguley, Andreas Göbel, Marcus Pappik, Leon Schiller

Abstract
We study high-dimensional random geometric graphs (RGGs) of edge-density p p with vertices uniformly distributed on the d d -dimensional torus and edges inserted between ‘sufficiently close’ vertices with respect to an L q L_{q} -norm. In this setting, we focus on distinguishing an RGG from an Erdős–Rényi graph if both models have the same marginal edge probability p p . So far, most results in the literature considered either spherical RGGs with L 2 L_{2} -distance or toroidal RGGs under L ∞ L_{\infty} -distance. However, for general L q L_{q} -distances, many questions remain open, especially if p p is allowed to depend on n n . The main reason for this is that RGGs under L q L_{q} -distances can not easily be represented as the logical ‘AND’ of their 1-dimensional counterparts, as is the case for L ∞ L_{\infty} geometries. To overcome this difficulty, we devise a novel technique for quantifying the dependence between edges based on a modified version of Edgeworth expansions. Our technique yields the first tight algorithmic upper bounds for distinguishing toroidal RGGs under general L q L_{q} norms from Erdős–Rényi graphs for any fixed p p and q q . We achieve this by showing that the signed triangle statistic can distinguish the two models when d ≪ n 3 ​ p 3 d\ll n^{3}p^{3} for the whole regime of edge probabilities c n < p < 1 \frac{c}{n}<p<1 . Additionally, our technique yields an improved information-theoretic lower bound for this task, showing that the two distributions converge in total variation whenever d = Ω ~ ​ ( n 3 ​ p 2 ) d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3}p^{2}) , which is just as strong as the currently best known lower bound for spherical RGGs in case of general p p from Liu et al. [STOC’22]. Finally, our expansions allow us to tightly characterize the spectral properties of toroidal RGGs both under L q L_{q} -distances for fixed 1 ⩽ q < ∞ 1\leqslant q<\infty , and L ∞ L_{\infty} -distance. We find that these are quite different for q < ∞ q<\infty vs. q = ∞ q=\infty . Our results partially resolve a conjecture of Bangachev and Bresler [COLT’24] and prove that the distance metric, rather than the underlying space, is responsible for the observed differences in the behavior of high-dimensional spherical and toroidal RGGs.



Accepted for presentation at the
Conference on Learning Theory (COLT) 2025

1
Introduction

Random geometric graphs (RGGs) are a cornerstone for the mathematical modeling of a wide range of real-world phenomena.
While they were first introduced by Gilbert
[
18
]
to model communication networks of radio stations, RGGs have found applications in various settings including protein interactions
[
20
]
, opinion dynamics
[
14
]
and motion planning
[
30
]
.
In particular RGGs have been proven to reproduce numerous properties of real-world networks
[
1
,
17
]
, making them a valuable tool in various disciplines.
Moreover, they were shown to be well-suited to represent high-dimensional feature vectors, encountered in statistical mechanics
[
19
]
and in data science
[
13
]
.

Given a metric space
(\Omega,\rho)
, a probability measure
\mu
over
\Omega
, a size
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a connection threshold
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,
a
random geometric graph

\mathbf{G}

comprises vertices
v\in[n]\coloneqq\{1,\dots,n\}
with independent position vectors
\mathbf{x}_{v}\sim\mu
that are connected if and only if their distance (as defined by
\rho
) is below
\tau
.
Formally,
\mathbf{G}

has the following distribution:
for any undirected graph
G
with adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{G}=G\right)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}_{v}\sim\mu,\\
v\in[n]\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{1\leqslant u<v\leqslant n}\left(\mathds{1}\left(\rho(\mathbf{x}_{u},\mathbf{x}_{v})\leqslant\tau\right)\right)^{A_{u,v}}\left(\mathds{1}\left(\rho(\mathbf{x}_{u},\mathbf{x}_{v})>\tau\right)\right)^{1-A_{u,v}}\right].

We denote the law of
\mathbf{G}
by
\mathrm{RGG}_{\rho}(n,\Omega,p,\mu)
, where
p\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\left(\rho(\mathbf{x}_{u},\mathbf{x}_{v})\leqslant\tau)\right)
is the connection probability.

Depending on the parametrization of the model, RGGs can differ significantly from the classical random graph model of Erdős–Rényi graphs (denoted by
G\left(n,p\right)
), where edges are sampled independently with probability
p
. For example, when the underlying metric space is not homogeneous (e.g., a
d
-dimensional hypercube), Dall and Christensen
[
10
]
have shown that for any choice of
d
, RGGs differ significantly from the
G\left(n,p\right)
model. However, when the metric space is homogeneous (e.g.,
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
or
\mathbb{T}^{d}
) with a natural notion of dimensionality, it has been shown that edges lose their dependency and RGGs converge to Erdős–Rényi graphs as
d\to\infty

[
16
,
11
]
.
This observation raises the question of how to determine the values of
d
for which the two distributions differ, or as Duchemin and De Castro
[
12
]
phrase it, “determine the point where geometry is lost in the dimension.”
The main focus of this article is to
identify the values of
d
for which we can distinguish—via an efficient statistical test—between the hypotheses

H_{0}:\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad H_{1}:\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\rho}(n,\Omega,p,\mu).

This question is relatively well-understood for
(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},L_{2})
, i.e. the surface of the
d
-dimensional unit sphere with the
L_{2}
-norm, in which the position vectors associated with the vertices are sampled from the uniform measure. Research in this direction was initiated by Devroye et al.
[
11
]
, who showed that the clique number is indistinguishable from that of the
G\left(n,p\right)
when
d=\Omega(\log^{3}n)
, and also that the two models are completely indistinguishable when
d\gg\exp(n^{2})
.
To the best of our knowledge, the first algorithmic results on testing for geometry were given by Bubeck et al.
[
8
]
, showing a threshold of
d=\Theta(n^{3})
for the dense regime of
p=\Theta(1)
when
d=\mathrm{poly}(n)
. In particular, when
d=\omega(n^{3})
the total variation distance of the two graph distributions goes to 0 as
n\to\infty
, while when
d\in o(n^{3})
, the number of signed triangles serves as a good statistic for detecting geometry. In addition, Bubeck et al.
[
8
]
showed that for the sparse regime of
p=o(1)
, when
d=o(\log^{3}n)
, signed triangles distinguish RGGs from Erdős–Rényi graphs. Subsequently, Brennan, Bresler, and Nagaraj
[
7
]
obtained an upper bound on the testing threshold by showing that the two random graph models are statistically indistinguishable for
d=\Omega(n^{3/2})
. Most recently, Liu et al.
[
26
]
improved the indistinguishability bound to
d=\Omega(\log^{36}n)
.

Setting aside the polylogarithmic gap in the above threshold, a consequent open question—see also the survey
(
12
, Section 3.4, Question 3)
—is how the geometry detection thresholds behave when other metric spaces are used to sample the vertices in RGGs.
Since we believe that the indistinguishability result in
[
10
]
generalizes to other non-homogeneous metric spaces, the question becomes more interesting for homogeneous metric spaces.
1
1

1

For our purposes, we call a metric space
(\Omega,d)
equipped with the Borel algebra
\mathcal{F}
and a measure
\mu

homogeneous
if, for all radii
r\geqslant 0
, a ball of radius
r
under distance
d
has the same measure regardless of its center
x\in\Omega
.

In this article we focus on the
d
-dimensional torus with
L_{q}
-distances, for any fixed
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}
(i.e.,
(\Omega,\rho)=(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{q})
).
More formally, the vertices of the RGG are associated with vectors in
[-1/2,1/2]^{d}
and their distance is given by

\|\mathbf{x}_{u}-\mathbf{x}_{v}\|_{q}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(|\mathbf{x}_{u}(i)-\mathbf{x}_{v}(i)|_{C})^{q})^{\frac{1}{q}}
,
where
|x-y|_{C}\coloneqq\min\{|x-y|,1-|x-y|\}
is the distance on the unit circle.
We write
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
for the associated law, where, as before,
p\in[0,1]
uniquely determines the threshold value
\tau
such that each pair of vertices is connected by an edge with probability
p
.

The first to study high-dimensional RGGs with
(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{q})
as underlying space were Friedrich, et. al.
[
16
]
who established that
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and Erdős–Rényi graphs converge in total variation as
d\to\infty
(and their inhomogeneous variants), for all fixed values of
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}
—this includes the maximum norm
L_{\infty}
.
In subsequent work, the same authors
[
17
]
showed that a triangle statistic can be used to determine the dimensionality of an RGG for
(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{\infty})
, when
d=o(\log n)
. Bangachev and Bresler
[
2
]
showed that counting (signed) triangles is not the optimal statistic for detecting geometry in
(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{\infty})
, when
d=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, which is surprising because it is for the sphere. They consider the whole edge probability regime of
n^{-1}<p\leqslant 1/2
and show that the two distributions are indistinguishable when
d=\tilde{\omega}(\max\{n^{3/2}p,n\})
. Additionally they showed that the signed triangle statistic distinguishes the two graph distributions when
d=\tilde{o}((np)^{3/4})
, while the signed 4-cycle statistic does so when
d=\tilde{o}(np)
, and moreover that the later statistic is optimal in some sense
(
2
, Theorem 1.5)
.

All aforementioned results on geometry testing with
(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},L_{2})
and
(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{\infty})
use the fact that that the metrics factorize over coordinates. In
(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},L_{2})
, for example,
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}=\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|\cdot|y_{i}|\cdot\sign(x_{i},y_{i})
, in which case
\sign(x_{i},y_{i})
is a one-dimensional edge independent from the other coordinates and the
|x_{i}|\cdot|y_{i}|
are weights. Furthermore, in
(\mathbb{T}^{d},L_{\infty})
,
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\tau
if for all
i\in[d]
, it holds that
|x_{i}-y_{i}|_{C}\leqslant\tau
. This has been a crucial component in all analyses so far: as Bangachev and Bresler
(
2
, Section 1.2.1)
admit “the analysis of
L_{q}
models, however, turns out to be much more difficult when
q<\infty
as the factorization over 1-dimensional random geometric graphs does not hold any longer. In particular, this makes the
computation of signed subgraph counts much more difficult.”
Despite this technical challenge those authors were able to obtain results for the special case of
p=1/2
for all values of
q
. We highlight their results for
q\in o(d/\log d)
: in that case
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,1/2\right)
is indistinguishable from
G(n,1/2)
when
dq=\tilde{\omega}(n^{3})
and the distributions of the two models diverge for all
1/n\leqslant p\leqslant 1/2
, when
d=o(np/logn)
. Furthermore, they conjecture that the signed triangle statistic is able to distinguish
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,1/2\right)
from
G(n,1/2)
only when
dq\in\tilde{o}(n^{3})

(
2
, Conjecture A.3)
.

1.1
Our Results

We advance the understanding of high-dimensional
(
d=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, precisely, we assume throughout this paper that
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
for
\gamma>0
arbitrarily small)
random geometric graphs on the torus under an
L_{q}
-norm (where
q<\infty
is fixed) and for the whole range of edge-densities
n^{-O\left(1\right)}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
. Specifically, we devise a novel technique for quantifying the dependence between the coordinates of a given random vector, which, as we explain in the following, allows us to overcome the hurdle of
L_{q}
norms don’t factorize to one-dimensional components when
q<\infty
. Using this approach, we obtain the following results.

Tight testing thresholds via signed triangles.

Our first result is a tight algorithmic threshold for the signed triangle statistic in case that
p=\Theta(1)
. It also yields improved algorithmic upper bounds for
p=o(1)
. We define the number of signed triangles in a graph
\mathbf{G}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\coloneqq\sum_{i<j<k\in[n]}\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\text{ where }\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\coloneqq(\mathbf{G}_{ij}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{ik}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{jk}-p).

where
\mathbf{G}_{ij}
denotes the indicator variable of the edge
\{i,j\}
. With this, we prove the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
,
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
and any
\frac{\alpha}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
for constants
\epsilon,\alpha>0
, the number of signed triangles distinguishes
\mathbf{G}_{1}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}\sim G\left(n,p\right)
with probability
1-o(1)
whenever
d=o(n^{3}p^{3})
, and fails at doing so whenever
d=\tilde{\omega}(n^{3}p^{3})
.
Precisely,

\displaystyle\left|\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]\right|-\max\left\{\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]},\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]}\right\}=\begin{cases}\omega(1)&\text{if }d=o(n^{3}p^{3})\\
o(1)&\text{if }d=\tilde{\omega}(n^{3}p^{3}).\end{cases}

This result improves upon the upper bound given by Theorem 1.11 of
[
2
]
, which only asserts that the total variation distance
tends to
1
(i.e.,
\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)=1-o(1)
)
if
d=\tilde{o}(np)
.
Their result does not rely on signed triangles but instead uses an entropy-based argument, which is built upon an explicit
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with specific properties. This has the further drawback of being an existential arguments which does not yield a computationally efficient test.
2
2

2

However, it works even if
q
is allowed to depend on
n
.

For any fixed
q
,
Theorem

1.1
yields a test that works in a wider range of possible values for
d
and
p
, and is further computationally efficient.
At the same time, it proves
(
2
, Conjecture A.3)
for fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
and matches the information-theoretic threshold given by
(
2
, Theorem 1.10)
if
p=\Theta(1)
(up to polylogarithmic factors). We remark that
Theorem

1.1
yields the same computational power as the signed triangle test on spherical RGGs under
L_{2}
-norm, as proved in
[
26
]
.

Improved information-theoretic lower bounds for all densities.

In addition to improved algorithmic upper bounds, we also obtain an improved information-theoretic lower bounds for distinguishing
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
from
G\left(n,p\right)
that works for all relevant edge-densities
p
.

Theorem 1.2
.

For any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty,\alpha>0
and any
\frac{\alpha}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
, we have that the total variation distance
\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)=o(1)
whenever
d=\omega(\max\{n\log(n),p^{2}n^{3}\log^{6}(n)\})
.

Theorem

1.2
improves over the previously known information-theoretic lower bound for distinguishing toroidal RGGs under
L_{q}
-norm from a
G\left(n,p\right)
, given by
(
2
, Theorem 1.10)
, which only applies for
p=1/2
(but allows for
q=\omega(1)
). This bound is based on an intricate combination of anti-concentration and Fourier-theoretic methods but does not easily generalize to the case of
p=o(1)
. Our bound does not have this limitation and yields the same threshold as the currently best known lower bound for
(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},L_{2})
from
[
26
]
, while relying on entirely different techniques and extending to a more general regime of possible distance functions (all
L_{q}
-norms instead of only
L_{2}
-norm).
3
3

3

We remark that
[
26
]
proves a stronger lower bound for spherical RGGs in case
p=\alpha/n
for constant
\alpha
that relies on the locally tree-like properties of sparse, spherical RGGs. However, these techniques break down if
p=\omega(1/n)
. In this case, their lower-bound is just as strong as the one of
Theorem

1.2
.
We find it remarkable that the underlying technical heart of the above results is powerful enough to yield improved algorithmic upper
and
lower-bounds that are precisely as strong as the currently best known bounds for spherical RGGs in the case of general
p
, which rely on quite different techniques. Precisely, the lower-bound in
[
25
]
uses concentration bounds for the intersection of random spherical caps and anti-caps via transportation inequalities, and the upper bound from
[
25
]
and
[
8
]
relies on a more direct integration that makes heavy use of the spherical symmetry. We generalize both of these results to a wider class of possible distance functions while relying on the same underlying way of quantifying dependencies between edges for both our upper- and lower-bound.

Spectral Properties of
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
.

Finally, quantifying the dependence between edges allows us to tightly (i.e. up to lower order factors) characterize the spectrum of a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
both for fixed
q<\infty
and
q=\infty
. Spectral properties of spherical RGGs have recently gained a lot of attention in a series of works
[
3
,
4
,
24
,
27
]
, where they were further used algorithmically for latent vector recovery, community detection, and robust testing.
A key takeaway from these works (in particular from
[
24
]
) is that the adjacency matrix of a spherical RGG has
\tilde{\Theta}(d)
large eigenvalues, each of order
\tilde{\Theta}(np/\sqrt{d})
, provided that
d\ll np
, while all remaining eigenvalues are of lower order.
Furthermore, these
d
eigenvectors are close to the
d
rows of the
d\times n
matrix
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})
, where
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the latent vector associated to vertex
i
. On the other hand, if
d\gg np
, the second largest eigenvalue is of order
\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{np})
, as in a
G\left(n,p\right)
.
Accordingly,
d=np
marks the so-called
spectral threshold
, that is,
a spherical RGG with
d\ll np

is spectrally very different from a
G\left(n,p\right)
, while for
d\gg np
both models are spectrally similar. These observations (in case
d\ll np
) were also used for designing efficient spectral algortihms for latent vector recovery and clustering in
[
24
]
, and they also constitute the foundation of the sum-of-squares based spectral refutation algorithm for distinguishing an RGG from a
G\left(n,p\right)
under a small constant fraction of adversarial edge corruptions from
[
4
]
. Our technique yields a similar characterization of the spectrum of toroidal RGGs and extends the existing results known for the sphere under
L_{2}
norm to all
1\leqslant q<\infty
.

Theorem 1.3

(Spectral properties of
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
have adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})
. For any
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
and any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
, there is a constant
C>0
such that with probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle C\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}\leqslant\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}\leqslant n^{o(1)}\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}.

Moreover, in case
d=\tilde{o}(np)
, there are
\tilde{\Omega}(d)
eigenvalues of order
\Theta(np/\sqrt{d})
. If
d\leqslant np^{1+\varepsilon}
, then for any
a\geqslant 1
, the number of eigenvalues of magnitude
\geqslant\frac{np}{a\sqrt{d}}
is at most
\widetilde{O}(da^{4+2/\varepsilon})
.

Theorem

1.3
shows that a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
for any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
has similar spectral properties to a spherical RGG (with
L_{2}
-norm) and, in particular, obeys the same spectral threshold at
d\approx np
.
Furthermore, the theorem shows that when
d\ll np
,
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
has at most
d^{1+o(1)}
large eigenvalues of order
\Omega(np/\sqrt{d})
, while all the remaining
\Theta(n)
eigenvalues are of order
o(np/\sqrt{d})
and therefore of lower order. As in the case of a spherical RGG, we can explicitly construct
d
nearly orthogonal eigenvectors with associated eigenvalue
\Omega(np/\sqrt{d})
by defining one vector per dimension, based on the latent positions of all vertices in said dimension. We refer the reader to
Section

8.3
for all details underlying this construction.
On a high level, each such vector is defined over
\{0,\pm 1\}^{n}
and supported on vertices corresponding to two opposing circular arcs in a fixed dimension. Coordinates corresponding to vertices in the first arc are all set to
1
, while coordinate corresponding to the second arc are
-1
. Since vertices within the same arc have a slightly larger probability of being adjacent than other pairs, this yields the desired properties. To analyze how these slightly perturbed connection probabilities, we also use (univariate) Edgeworth expansions.

A different bound for
L_{\infty}
-norm

A simplification of the techniques used to prove
Theorem

1.3
further allows us to study the spectral properties of
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
. Here, the spectrum behaves rather differenctly differently.

Theorem 1.4

(Spectral properties of
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
have adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})
. For any
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
, there is a constant
C>0
such that with probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle C\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{d}\right\}\leqslant\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}\leqslant\log(n)^{11}\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{d}\right\}.

Moreover, in case
d=\tilde{o}(\sqrt{np})
, there are
\tilde{\Omega}(d^{2})
eigenvalues of order
\Theta(np/d)
. If
d\leqslant p^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{np}
, then for any
a
, the number of eigenvalues of magnitude
\geqslant\frac{np}{ad}
is at most
\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}a^{4+1/\varepsilon}\right)
.

Theorem

1.4
shows that a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
obeys a spectral threshold at
d\approx\sqrt{np}
instead of
d\approx np
. Furthermore, for
d\ll\sqrt{np}
,
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
typically has
O(d^{2+o(1)})
large eigenvalues of order
\Omega(np/d)
, while all remaining eigenvalues have order
o(np/d)
. As in the
q<\infty
case, we explicitly construct
\Theta(d^{2})
nearly orthogonal approximate eigenvectors with eigenvalue
\Theta(np/d)
, this time by finding roughly
d
such vectors per dimension. Each vector has a similar structure as in the
q<\infty
case, but is more sparse, which results in a larger number of vectors that can be constructed.

While we do not explicitly study algorithmic applications of the above results, we believe that they can be extended to yield efficient algorithms for similar problems as studied in
[
24
,
4
]
, namely recovery of latent vectors or an approximate distance matrix associated to a given
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
, clustering, and robust testing. In particular, we think that our explicit construction of approximate eigenvectors can serve as a certificate for underlying geometry that is robust to a certain number of adversarial edge-corruptions and can efficiently be checked for using a sum-of-squares based approach as in
[
4
]
. We think that a formal investigation of these ideas constitutes an interesting direction for future research.

1.2
Discussion and Outlook

While this work is focuses on
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
, the techniques are general and could prove helpful in understanding RGGs on different metric spaces, even when sampling vertices non-uniformly, we further discuss this on
Section

2.7
. Furthermore, we believe our approach to be applicable when
q=\omega(1)
, but this adaptation involves more cumbersome calculations and technicalities.

A promising direction for future research is to study the signed weight of more general patterns
H
. This was very recently achieved for spherical RGGs by
[
3
]
, however their techniques heavily rely on leveraging spherical symmetry and inner products, and thus an adaptation to the torus (even for
L_{2}
-norm) seems challenging. We believe a combination of our approach together with the ‘cluster expansion’ technique of
[
2
]
could be fruitful. Moreover, it would be interesting to find algorithmic applications related to the spectral properties of
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
. On the sphere, this was achieved by
[
24
]
for latent vector recovery, clustering, and community detection. Our explicit construction of eigenvectors could serve as an efficiently refutable certificate of geometry, as recently used in the context of robust testing for RGGs on the sphere
[
4
]
.

Finally, it would be interesting to improve upon the gap between information-theoretic lower bounds and algorithmic upper bounds in case of
p=o(1)
. This is open even for spherical RGGs, and resolving it would require a better understanding of ‘average-case’ versus ‘worst-case’ vector embeddings of a given
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
as already observed in
[
25
]
.

2
Technical Overview

This section contains an overview of the techniques used to prove
Theorems

1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4
; full proofs can be found in the subsequent sections. We introduce a novel technique for quantifying the dependence between coordinates of a particular class of random vectors. In our case, each entry of this vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
represents the (rescaled) distances associated to a collection
H=\{\{u_{1},v_{1}\},\ldots\{u_{k},v_{k}\}\}
of potential edges in an
RGG.
Accordingly,
\mathbf{z}
determines which edges in
H
are present in
\mathbf{G}
and which not, so dependencies between the entries of
\mathbf{z}
typically tell us about the dependencies between the edges in
H
. A crucial fact about
\mathbf{z}
is that it can be represented as a sum
\mathbf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{z}_{i}/\sqrt{d}
of independent (although not necessarily i.i.d.) vectors
\mathbf{z}_{i}
, one for each dimension in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
The
\mathbf{z}_{i}
contain the (centered and rescaled) distances associated to the vertex pairs from
H
in dimension
i
. While it is illustrative to think about
\mathbf{z}
in terms of this interpretation, we remark that our technique extends beyond this application.

Now, in each
\mathbf{z}_{i}
, there are certain dependencies between the individual coordinates that is ‘inherited’ by
\mathbf{z}
. For example, in case all the
\mathbf{z}_{i}
are independent Gaussian vectors
\mathbf{z}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma})
for some covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
, it follows that
\mathbf{z}
also has distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma})
and the dependencies between its coordinates are fully captured by all the off-diagonal entries in
\bm{\Sigma}
. More generally, it is intuitive that the
mixed moments
in multiple coordinates of
\mathbf{z}_{i}
naturally appearing in its Fourier transform should characterize how ‘interdependent’ the coordinates are. Again, in case of Gaussian vectors, the entries in
\bm{\Sigma}
are precisely all the mixed moments of order
2
, fully describing the dependencies within
\mathbf{z}
. However, when considering distributions other than the Gaussian, the entries of
\mathbf{z}
can be dependent while still being pairwise uncorrelated, implying that moments of order
2
(i.e. the covariances) contain no information about the dependencies of interest. In such cases, it is necessary to take higher order mixed moments into account.

2.1
Joint Cumulants Capture Dependencies

It is typically easier to work with
cumulants
instead of mixed moments, especially when dealing with the higher-order versions of such quantities. Cumulants are related to mixed moments, but obey much more convenient additive and homogeneous properties, which are especially useful when working with sums of independent random vectors. Cumulants are defined via the complex-valued
characteristic function
(CF)
C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(i(\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{z})\right)\right]
,
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
i=\sqrt{-1}
. Taking the logarithm yields the
cumulant generating function
(CGF)

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{z}}((t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})^{\mathsf{T}})=\log\left(C_{\mathbf{z}}((t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})^{\mathsf{T}})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})\frac{(it_{1})^{s_{1}}(it_{2})^{s_{2}}\cdots(it_{k})^{s_{k}}}{s_{1}!s_{2}!\cdots s_{k}!},

where
\mathbb{N}^{\times k}
is the set of all
k
-tuples over the natural numbers, and the quantities
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})
are the
cumulants
of
\mathbf{z}
. It is further convenient to define a ‘vectorized’ version of the CGF by setting
K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}
where
\bm{\kappa}_{j}
is a vector containing all (suitably rescaled) cumulants.

‘Pure’ and ‘mixed’ cumulants.

There is a simple intuition for why certain cumulants characterize the dependencies between the entries of
\mathbf{z}
, which is obtained by comparing the individual (univariate) cumulants of the entries of
\mathbf{z}
to the joint cumulants of
\mathbf{z}
. If the entries of
\mathbf{z}
were all independent, then the CGF of
\mathbf{z}
would have special structure, captured in the following observation.

Observation 2.1

(CGFs are additive for independent coordinates)
.

Assume that
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a random vector in which all coordinates
\{\mathbf{z}(j)\}_{j\in[k]}
are independent. Then,

K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}K_{\mathbf{z}(j)}(\mathbf{t}(j))
.

Observation

2.1
shows that the CGF of the vector
\mathbf{z}
is just the sum of the CGFs of its individual entries if these are independent. Another way to phrase this is to say that
K_{\mathbf{z}}
has the form

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{(i\mathbf{t}(j))^{s}}{s!}\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z}(j)),

which implies that
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})=0
whenever
s=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})
has more than two non-zero coordinates. This suggests a partitioning of the cumulants of
\mathbf{z}
into two classes.

Definition 2.2

(Pure and mixed cumulants)
.

For some
s=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})
, we call the cumulant
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})

pure
if exactly one of the
s_{i}
is non-zero, and
mixed
otherwise.

Pure cumulants are exactly those that also appear in the CGFs of the individual entries of
\mathbf{z}
, while mixed cumulants only appear in
K_{\mathbf{z}}
. Since
Observation

2.1
shows that all mixed cumulants are zero if the entries of
\mathbf{z}
are independent, this suggests that perturbations from
0
in the mixed cumulants of
\mathbf{z}
quantify the dependence between individual entries.

Mixed cumulants capture deviations from the ‘ground state’.

A different way to make sense of
Observation

2.1
is to view pure cumulants as those describing the ‘ground state’ of the vector
\mathbf{z}
in which all entries are distributed according to their marginal distribution in
\mathbf{z}
, independently of each other.
On the other hand, all the mixed cumulants of a given order
r
(i.e. all mixed cumulants
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})
for
s=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})
with
s_{1}+s_{2}+\ldots=r
) capture the ‘
r
-th order deviation’ from this ground state. All such deviations combined contain all information regarding the dependence between multiple coordinates. Conveniently, due to the homogeneity of cumulants in case of sums of independent random vectors such as
\mathbf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{z}_{i}/\sqrt{d}
, cumulants of order
r
are typically of magnitude
\approx(1/\sqrt{d})^{r-2}
, so they rapidly decay in
r
.
Intuitively, this
suggests that the lowest-order non-zero mixed cumulants should contain the largest amount of information about dependencies.

Mixed cumulants for cycles and chains.

It turns out that this point of view is particularly helpful when trying to account for the dependencies between edges in a cycle or a chain of vertices appearing in a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
.
Assume that
\mathbf{z}
represents the distances associated to a cycle
H
of length
k
,
i.e., assume there is a sequence of fixed vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
in
\mathbf{G}
such that
H=\{\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\}\mid j\in[k]\}
, and
\mathbf{z}_{i}(j)=(\Delta_{i}(\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\})-\mu)/\sigma
represents the distance associated to
\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\}
in dimension
i
,
where
\mu,\sigma
are such that
\mathbf{z}_{i}(j)
has mean
0
and variance
1
.
In this case, for every proper subset
S\subset[k]
of coordinates, the random variables
\{\mathbf{z}_{i}(j)\}_{j\in S}
correspond to the distances associated to the edges in a forest (since deleting any edge from a cycle yields a forest), and in this setting all the distances are independent. By the relationship between cumulants and mixed moments (cf. (
9
)), this forces all mixed cumulants of order
<k
and all cumulants of order
k
except for
\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z})
to be
0
. The same holds if
H
represents a chain with endpoints
u,v
, conditional on the fixed positions of
u,v
. This is captured in the following observation.

Observation 2.3

(Mixed cumulants for cycles and chains)
.

Assuming that
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
represents a cycle or a chain with
k
edges, all mixed cumulants of order
\leqslant k
except for
\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z})
are zero. In case of a chain, this even holds conditional on the position of the endpoints
u,v
of the chain.

Per the discussion above, this suggests that
\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z})
characterizes the highest-order perturbations from the ‘ground state’ and thus captures most of the dependence between the edges of
H
.

While
Observation

2.3
is tailored towards cycles and chains, we remark that it also helps us understand the dependencies between edges in any pattern
H
that merely contain cycles and chains, which we crucially use for characterizing the spectrum of
\mathbf{G}
.In the following, we sketch how to turn
Observation

2.3
into an actual quantification of the mentioned dependencies.

2.2
Classical Edgeworth Expansions

A common approach to quantifying the influence of cumulants on the distribution of a random variable is an
Edgeworth Expansion
, the underlying idea of which is to apply a Fourier inversion to an approximation of the CF of
\mathbf{z}
obtained by using a suitable Taylor series. This has the advantage that—after applying Fourier inversion—the leading term is equal to the density of a standard Gaussian (denoted by
\phi
) plus a series of correction terms, explicitly phrased in terms of cumulants of order
\geqslant 3
and derivatives of
\phi
(or alternatively in terms of Hermite polynomials). More precisely,
C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\exp\left(K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})\right)
is approximated by

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}+\sum_{j=3}^{s}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}\Big)=\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}\Big)\exp\Big(\sum_{j=3}^{b}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}\Big)

where
b\geqslant 3
is a parameter
governing how many cumulants are taken into account. Above, we used that the first term in
K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})
is
0
when centering
\mathbf{z}
so that it has mean
0
, and that the second term is
\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}
when rescaling
\mathbf{z}
to have identity covariance. Then, after applying an
(s-2)
-order Taylor expansion to the second exponential appearing above, we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})\approx\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}\Big)\sum_{\ell=0}^{s-2}\Big(\sum_{j=3}^{s}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}\Big)^{\ell}\!\!\!=\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}\Big)+\sum_{j=3}^{s(s-2)}\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}\Big)\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}

for a collection of vectors
\bm{\alpha}_{j}
that depend on cumulants of order
\geqslant 3
. Applying the above transformation has the advantage that the expression can now explicitly be Fourier inverted so that the first term
\exp(-\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}/2)
becomes exactly the density
\phi
of a standard Gaussian, and every subsequent term becomes a multiple of
\phi
involving the
\bm{\alpha}_{j}
and a product of Hermite polynomials. Collecting terms of roughly the same magnitude and defining an appropriate ‘vectorized’ version of Hermite polynomials
H_{j}
as done in
[
23
]
, one obtains approximate densities
such as

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\phi(\mathbf{x})\Big(1+\frac{\bm{\kappa}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}}{6\sqrt{d}}H_{3}(\mathbf{x})\Big),\text{ or}

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\phi(\mathbf{x})\Big(1+\frac{\bm{\kappa}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}}{6\sqrt{d}}H_{3}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{d}\Big(\frac{\bm{\kappa}_{4}^{\mathsf{T}}}{24}H_{4}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{\bm{\kappa}_{3}^{\otimes 2\mathsf{T}}}{72}H_{6}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\Big),

using an order
3
and order
4
expansion, respectively. We refer the interested reader to
[
23
]
for further details on this approach.

2.3
The Need for Specialized Expansions

In principle, the expansions in
Section

2.2
capture all information about dependence between the coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
, provided that the parameter
s
is chosen sufficiently large.
However,
classical Edgeworth expansions turn out to be impractical for quantifying
the influence of
mixed
cumulants on top of
\mathbf{z}
’s ‘ground state’ dictated by all the
pure
cumulants.
This is for two reasons.

First, in classical expansions, pure and mixed cumulants are not treated separately; instead many terms (especially those of higher order) contain products of pure and mixed cumulants, making it difficult to quantify the influence of one on the other. Secondly, the ‘ground state’ considered by classical expansions is not identical to the one we consider. Instead, the first term is always the density of a standard Gaussian, and subsequent terms are corrections to this ground state While our ground state is roughly Gaussian (by a CLT), this crucially only holds approximately and replacing the true ground state of
\mathbf{z}
by a Gaussian therefore introduces an additional source of error whose magnitude is non-negligible when compared to the small perturbations caused by mixed cumulants we wish to measure.

Fortunately, there is a more elegant and more direct approach to obtaining an approximate density capturing the correct ‘ground state’ and using correction terms directly linked to the mixed cumulants of
\mathbf{z}
, which we describe in the following section.
We remark that, while it might in principle be possible to obtain a result comparable to ours by relying on the expansions given in previous work
[
23
]
and ‘untangling’ and rearranging terms, the technical complexity of a such approach would be comparable (or worse), while being less illustrative and interpretable.
Moreover, since the result in
[
23
]
holds under more restrictive assumptions than here,
4
4

4

Specifically, summands are i.i.d., the CFs are integrable, and all terms satisfy Cramér’s condition.
certain technical modifications (see
Section

2.5
) would have to be worked into their proof in any case.

2.4
Main Theorem—Approximating the Density of
\mathbf{z}
above its Ground State

Our approach to approximating the CF of
\mathbf{z}
yields – after a Fourier inversion – an approximate density related directly to the true ground state of
\mathbf{z}
, that is more convenient than those in
Section

2.2
.
The ‘ground state’ of
\mathbf{z}
is defined as the density associated to the product measure
\otimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{z}(j))

over the laws of the entries of
\mathbf{z}
.
Alternatively, we can
directly define

f_{\textup{ind}}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\mathbf{z}(j)}(\mathbf{x}(j))
,
where
f_{\mathbf{z}(j)}
is the (marginal) density of
\mathbf{z}(j)
.
We further define

\kappa\coloneqq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z}_{j})
to be the average of all cumulants of order
(1,1,\ldots,1)
over the individual
\mathbf{z}_{j}
.
In case
\mathbf{z}
represents a cycle
H
, all these cumulants are equal and
\kappa=\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z}_{j})
for any
j
.
However, in case of a chain
H
conditional on the positions of its endpoints, the
terms are not all the same,
and
\kappa
is a function of the position of the two endpoints associated to
H
.
Nevertheless, in both cases the approximate joint density of
\mathbf{z}
can be expressed in terms of
\kappa
.
This is formally captured in our main technical result.

Theorem 2.4

(Main theorem, the joint density of
\mathbf{z}
above its ground state)
.

Let
H
be a cycle or a chain of length
k\geqslant 2
. Let
f
denote the density of
\mathbf{z}
and assume that a constant fraction of the
\mathbf{z}_{i}
satisfy Cramér’s condition (
Definition

2.5
) for some
\varepsilon(\delta)
.
Then, for any choice of integer valued parameters
b_{\textup{pure}}\geqslant 3,b_{\textup{mix}}\geqslant k,a_{\textup{pure}},a_{\textup{mix}}\geqslant 1
, there is a sequence of vectors
\left(\bm{\alpha}_{j}\right)_{j=k+1}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}
with
\bm{\alpha}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{j}}
such that the density
f
of
\mathbf{z}
is approximated by

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq f_{\textup{ind}}(\mathbf{x})+(-1)^{k}\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}(j))+\sum_{j=k+1}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\bm{\alpha}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)

where
\phi(\mathbf{x})
is the density of the standard Gaussian distribution and
\phi^{(1)}
is its first derivative. The approximation error satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|f(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})|=O\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}},|\kappa|\right\}^{\!a_{\textup{mix}}+1}\!\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\!(k-2)(a_{\textup{mix}}+1)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\!\min\left\{a_{\textup{pure}}+1,b_{\textup{pure}}-2,b_{\textup{mix}}-2\right\}}\right).

In case of a chain of length
k=2
, we require
|\kappa|=o(1)
for the above to hold. Furthermore, the absolute value of the coefficients in the remainder terms
\bm{\alpha}_{j}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\|\bm{\alpha}_{k+j}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\begin{cases}\max\left\{|\kappa|d^{-\frac{1}{6}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}d^{-\frac{j-1}{6}}&\textup{if }k\geqslant 3\text{ or }(k=2\text{ and }|\kappa|<d^{-\frac{1}{3}}),\\
\max\left\{|\kappa|^{1+\frac{j}{2}},d^{-\frac{j-1}{6}}\right\}&\textup{if }k=2\text{ and }|\kappa|\geqslant d^{-\frac{1}{3}},\end{cases}

where
C>0
is a constant.

In
Theorem

2.4
the higher order corrections are represented not in terms of Hermite polynomials, but in terms of (vectors of) partial derivatives of
\phi
. We believe this simplifies the exposition, but remark that it is simple to obtain an equivalent formulation involving Hermite polynomials instead.

We now sketch the proof of
Theorem

2.4
and explain the technical difficulties.
The key idea is to split the CGF of
\mathbf{z}
into ‘pure’ and ‘mixed’ cumulants as

K(\mathbf{t})=K_{(\textup{pure})}(\mathbf{t})+K_{(\textup{mix})}(\mathbf{t})
, where
K_{(\textup{pure})}
and
K_{(\textup{mix})}
, contain precisely those terms in the power series representation of
K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})
which correspond to pure or mixed cumulants, respectively.
To quantify the influence of
\kappa
on the distribution of
\mathbf{z}
, we define an approximation to the CGF and the CF of
\mathbf{z}
that takes all pure cumulants and the first mixed cumulant into account.

For this
we introduce the parameters
b_{\textup{pure}},b_{\textup{mix}}
as mentioned in
Theorem

2.4
and define

\displaystyle K_{(\textup{pure})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{pure}}}(\mathbf{t})\!=\!\!\sum_{j=3}^{b_{\textup{pure}}}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{(\textup{pure})\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}\text{ and }K_{(\textup{mix})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{mix}}}(\mathbf{t})\!=\!\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(i\mathbf{t}(j))\!+\!\!\!\!\sum_{j=k+1}^{b_{\textup{mix}}}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{(\textup{mix})\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}.

That is,
K_{(\textup{pure})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{pure}}}
contains all terms corresponding to pure cumulants of order between
3
and
b_{\textup{pure}}
while
K_{(\textup{mix})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{mix}}}
contains all terms corresponding to mixed cumulants of order between
k
and
b_{\textup{mix}}
. Then the CF of
\mathbf{z}
is approximated as

\displaystyle C(\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle\approx\exp\left(K_{(\text{pure})}(\mathbf{t})\right)+\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}{2}\Big)\exp\left(K_{(\textup{pure})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{pure}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)\left(\exp\left(K_{(\textup{mix})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{mix}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)-1\right),

leaving out all pure cumulants of order
>b_{\textup{pure}}
and all mixed cumulants of order
>b_{\textup{mix}}
in the second term.
To transform this expression into a sum of tractable correction terms,
we expand the exponentials as Taylor series of a certain order.
By applying an
a_{\textup{pure}}
-th order expansion to the first, and a
a_{\textup{mix}}
-th order expansion to the second term, we obtain

\displaystyle\exp\left(K_{(\textup{pure})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{pure}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)\approx\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=0}^{s_{1}}\frac{1}{\ell!}\left(K_{(\textup{pure})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{pure}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)^{\ell}}_{\eqqcolon P_{1}(\mathbf{t})}\text{ and }\exp\left(K_{(\textup{mix})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{mix}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)-1\approx\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{s_{2}}\frac{1}{\ell!}\left(K_{(\textup{mix})}^{\dagger b_{\textup{mix}}}(\mathbf{t})\right)^{\ell}}_{\eqqcolon P_{2}(\mathbf{t})}.

It is possible expand
P_{1},P_{2}
to end up with a complex polynomial of a particular form, similar in shape to the expansions used in
[
23
]
, which expansion is important in
Section

4.3.3
where the explicit bound on
\|\bm{\alpha}_{k+j}\|_{\infty}
is proved.
For now, it is only necessary to
note
that the product
P_{1}P_{2}
can be written in the form

\displaystyle P_{1}(\mathbf{t})P_{2}(\mathbf{t})=\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(i\mathbf{t}(j))+\sum_{j=k+1}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}

for a sequence of vectors
\left(\bm{\alpha}_{j}\right)_{j=k+1}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}
with
\bm{\alpha}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{j}}
for which the absolute value of all components can be explicitly bounded.
This leads to explicit bounds on the approximation to the CF of
\mathbf{z}
that can be Fourier inverted, eventually yielding
Theorem

2.4
.

2.5
Technical Challenges

The two major obstacles in the method described in
Section

2.4
are: (1) the individual
\mathbf{z}_{i}
have no density, and (2) a prerequisite of the inversion theorem (
Theorem

3.5
) is that the CF of
\mathbf{z}
is integrable, which is not guaranteed
a priori
. We briefly describe how to handle these obstacles.

Cramér’s condition ensures validity for vectors without density.

As already mentioned, the literature usually assumes the
\mathbf{z}_{i}
to have a density, which in our case is not given. To see this, consider the simple example in which
\mathbf{z}_{i}
represents the distances associated to a cycle of length
3
(in dimension
i
). Then, conditional on the first two coordinates
\mathbf{z}_{i}(1),\mathbf{z}_{i}(2)
, it holds that
\mathbf{z}_{i}(3)
can only take two specific
discrete
values, implying that
\mathbf{z}_{i}(1)
has no density.

However, a sum of independent random vectors that each have no density can itself have a density that can be approximated using the same tools as described above.
A sufficient criterion for this phenomenon to occur is Cramér’s condition.

Definition 2.5

(Cramér’s condition)
.

A random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies Cramér’s condition if for all
\delta>0
and all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\|\mathbf{t}\|\geqslant\delta
, we have
|C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})|\leqslant 1-\varepsilon(\delta),
where
\varepsilon(\delta)>0
for all
\delta>0
.

Cramér’s condition is commonly used in the literature as a prerequisite for Edgeworth expansions. While Cramér’s condition is always met for continuous random vectors (which follows from the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma), and while it is easy to construct examples of non-continuous random vectors that do not meet Cramér’s condition (e.g. lattice distributions), there are random vectors that have no density but still satisfy the above condition. Our
\mathbf{z}_{i}
are an example for this, which is formally shown in
Lemma

A.1
. This statement holds but it makes an exception for chains of length
k=2
. Here, we can only guarantee that Cramér’s condition holds with a constant probability over the draw of the endpoints of our chain. However, this still means that with extremely high probability, there will be a constant fraction of the terms in the sum satisfying Cramér’s condition, and this is enough to obtain the same error guarantees as claimed previously.

Adding Gaussian noise to ensure integrability of the CF.

The second obstacle is that both our error analysis in
Section

4.3.2
and the inversion theorem (
Theorem

3.5
) require a CF which is integrable.
To overcome this hurdle, we use a simple fix that consists of adding independent, isotropic Gaussian noise
\bm{\eta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,d^{-2\eta}\mathbf{I}_{k})
to
\mathbf{z}
, where
\eta
is a parameter. Since
\bm{\eta}
has an integrable CF, and since
\int|C_{\mathbf{z}+\bm{\eta}}(\mathbf{t})|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}=\int|C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})||C_{\bm{\eta}}(\mathbf{t})|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}\leqslant\int|C_{\bm{\eta}}(\mathbf{t})|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}
, this ensures integrability of the resulting
\mathbf{z}+\bm{\eta}
. Intuitively, taking the convolution of
\mathbf{z}
with an independent Gaussian random variable ensures that all densities involved are smooth and continuous. While this adds another source of error and requires careful attention in the error analysis in
Section

4.3.2
, it is quite easy to quantify by how much the probability of events defined in terms of the coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
is influenced by our noise (
Lemma

3.6
). In particular this additional error can be made small enough for our purposes when choosing
\eta
to be a sufficiently large constant. Furthermore, any constant
\eta
only leads to a ‘blow-up’ that is polynomial in
d
in
Section

4.3.2
, which is compensated by a factor exponentially small in
d
, arising due to Cramér’s condition.

2.6
Applications to
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)

We continue by describing how the expansions from
Theorem

2.4
are helpful for understanding
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
in high dimensions. A generally helpful quantity is the so-called
signed weight
of small pattern graphs
H
(represented as a set of edges), defined as

\textsc{Sw}(H)\coloneqq\prod_{e\in H}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)
,
where – given an edge
e=\{u,v\}
– we write
\mathds{1}(e)
for the indicator that the edge is present in a given
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
. The expected signed weight
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
, intuitively tells us something about the dependence between the edges in
H
. In fact, many recent breakthroughs in understanding random geometric graphs and their information-computation landscape as well as their spectral properties rely on computing the signed weight of suitable patterns graphs
H
(or alternatively the very closely related
Fourier Coefficients
). We mention
[
2
,
3
]
as very recent examples. Crucially, studying the signed weight of suitable patterns is helpful for both designing algorithmic upper and lower bounds (both computational and information-theoretic).

Bounding the signed weight of cycles and chains

Our three applications all rely on turning
Theorem

2.4
into a suitable bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
in case
H
is a cycle or a chain. As mentioned above, this bound is not limited to only cycles and chains, but also makes a statement about the signed weight of a pattern
H
in which cycles and chains appear. By a careful integration of the expansions in
Theorem

2.4
, we obtain the following bound.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
C_{k}
be a cycle of length
k\geqslant 3
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(C_{k})\right]=O\left(p^{k}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\right).

If
H
is a chain of length
k\geqslant 2
on the set of vertices
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k+1}\}
with positions
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, then conditional on any position
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{k+1}
of the endpoints of
H
,

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{k+1}\right]=O\left(p^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+p^{k}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\right).

where
\kappa\coloneqq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}(\mathbf{z}_{j})
(as in
Theorem

2.4
), which is a function of
\mathbf{x}_{1}
and
\mathbf{x}_{k+1}
. If
k=2
, we require
H
to be
(\alpha,d^{-1/3})
–good (cf.
Definition

4.1
) for an arbitrarily small constant
\alpha>0
.

The bound from
Theorem

2.6
(combined with further, specialized arguments) enables the aforementioned three applications regarding the properties of
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
.

2.6.1
Application 1: Tight Testing Thresholds via Signed Triangles

To prove
Theorem

1.1
, we analyze the statistical power of the
signed triangle
test on
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\coloneqq\sum_{i<j<k\in[n]}\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\text{ where }\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\coloneqq(\mathbf{G}_{ij}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{ik}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{jk}-p).

where
G_{ij}
denotes the indicator variable of the edge
\{i,j\}
.
We bound the
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})
from below, and its variance from above, then the result follows by Chebyshev’s inequality. While the variance bound follows from
Theorem

2.6
, the main challenge here is to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]

from below
. To this end we will use
Theorem

2.4
, however, in contrast to the upper bound on signed weights from
Theorem

2.6
we have to control the quantity
\kappa
much more carefully. In fact, we must assert that
\kappa
is a
negative
constant for all
q\geqslant 1
such that the first correction term from
Theorem

2.4
, proportional to
(-1)^{k}\kappa/\sqrt{d}
is positive which results in an increased probability of forming a triangle. To do this we rely on the following technical lemma, which we prove in
Section

A.3
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Consider a triangle
C_{3}
with vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
and edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
. For all
L_{q}
-norms with
q\geqslant 1
, we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]<0
where
\gamma(\{u,v\})\coloneqq|x_{u}-x_{v}|_{C}^{q}-\mathbb{E}\left[|x_{u}-x_{v}|_{C}^{q}\right]
and
x_{v_{1}},x_{v_{2}},x_{v_{3}}\sim\textup{Unif}\left(\left[-1/2,1/2\right]\right)
are the positions of
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
in a fixed dimension.

Proving the above relies on splitting
\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]\right]
where
y
is the distance associated to the endpoints of
e_{1}
and then employing a monotonicity argument to show that
\gamma(e_{1})
is strictly increasing in
y
and that
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]
is strictly decreasing in
y
. Intuitively, this asserts that the correlation of
\gamma(e_{2})
and
\gamma(e_{3})
is decreasing in
\gamma(e_{1})
, so
\gamma(e_{1})
and
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid\gamma(e_{1})]
are negatively correlated. The main technical challenge is to prove monotonicity of
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]
, which we achieve by carefully estimating the derivative of
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]
using the Leibnitz integral rule and suitable substitutions.

2.6.2
Application 2: Improved Information-Theoretic Lower Bounds for all Densities

Both our
Theorem

1.2
and
(
2
, Theorem 1.10)
are based on a bound from Liu and Ràcz
[
28
]
that relates the total variation distance between
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
G\left(n,p\right)
to a sum over the moments of the self-convolution of two specific edge indicator variables. Precisely, Liu and Ràcz showed that

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]\right),

where
\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}}[(\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})-p)(\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})-p)]

and
\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
denotes the indicator random variable for the event that
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\|_{q}\leqslant\tau
.
In
[
2
]
, the above moments of
\gamma
are bounded using the Bernstein–McDiarmid concentration inequality together with an involved combination of anti-concentration and Fourier theoretic arguments aimed at computing marginal increments and variances. While this allows to explicitly quantify the influence of
q
, adapting the heavy machinery underlying this proof to the case of general
p
seems out of reach. In contrast to this, we simply use a convenient expansion of the moments arising in the bound of Liu and Ràcz into a sum over signed weights of complete bipartite graphs
K_{2,k}
given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right],

such that we can subsequently apply our bound from
Theorem

2.6
ultimately resulting in the bound from
Theorem

1.2
. This is possible since a
K_{2,k}
is just the union of
k
chains of length
2
, so
Theorem

2.6
is applicable (modulo some technical precautions).

Comparison with the bounds known for spherical RGGs

On top of
Theorem

2.6
, all of this can be done in a remarkably short proof, while still resulting in a lower bound that is just as strong as the best known information-theoretic lower bound for spherical RGGs in the case of general
p
from
(
26
, Theorem 1.2)
. This is despite the fact that the bound in
[
26
]
relies on much heavier machinery based on concentration results of the measure of the intersection of random spherical caps and anti-caps using optimal transport. We remark that the authors of
[
26
]
also prove a stronger lower bound in case of
p=\alpha/n
for constant
\alpha>0
which relies on a specialized argument exploiting the locally-tree-like properties of sparse RGGs to analyze an instance of the belief propagation algorithm for computing the marginal distribution of latent vectors conditioned on forming a specific graph, reminiscent of the ‘cavity method’ from statistical physics. However, this analysis breaks down in the case of general
p
as the locally-tree-like properties are lost. In this case, their approach based on ‘concentration via optimal transport’ yields the same bound as in
Theorem

1.2
(up to polylogarithmic factors). Closing the gap between algorithmic upper bounds and information-theoretic lower bounds for all
p=o(1)
, i.e., the gap between
n^{3}p^{3}
and
n^{3}p^{2}
, is an open problem both for spherical RGGs and our model. It arises because all techniques for proving lower-bounds in the general
p
case rely on ‘worst-case’ vector embeddings of a given graph
\mathbf{G}
, and going beyond this barrier requires new approaches that allow for analyzing ‘average-case’ vector embeddings instead, this was also observed in
[
26
]
.

2.6.3
Application 3: Spectral Properties of Random Toroidal Graphs

The upper bound of
Theorem

1.3
and
Theorem

1.4
is proved via the trace method. Since we are interested in the second largest eigenvalue in absolute value, the matrix we shall consider is the
centered
adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}_{p}=\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, where
\mathds{1}
is the
n
-dimensional all-ones vector. This eliminates the eigenspace associated to the largest eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}
(which is well approximated by
\mathds{1}
). We thus reduced our problem to bounding the largest eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, which we do by bounding the trace of
\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}
for an even, positive integer
m
. This is helpful for bounding the spectral norm since

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

so considering the
m
-th power of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\text{, so }\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|^{m}\leqslant\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)

for every even
m
. By Markov’s inequality, this means that for any
a=\omega(1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\geqslant a^{1/m}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}\right)\leqslant 1/a=o(1)

(1)

and we are left with bounding the above expectation. Conveniently,
\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)
has a combinatorial interpretation as the sum over all directed, closed walks of length
m
on the complete graph
K_{n}
with vertex set
[n]
, while each term in the sum corresponds to the product of the entries of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
along the edges of the walk.
Formally, writing
\textsc{Ew}(n,m)\coloneqq\{(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in[n]^{m+1}\mid v_{1}=v_{m+1}\}
for the set of closed walks of length
m
, we have

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)=\sum_{(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\big(\mathbf{A}_{v_{j},v_{j+1}}-p\big).

(2)

Now it is easy to see that the right hand side of the above can be represented as a sum over signed weights of suitable Eulerian multigraphs.
To this end, we associate a multigraph
H_{\boldsymbol{v}}
to a given walk
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)
, defined as the multigraph on the vertex set
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1}\}
(duplications of vertices are removed), where we add an edge
\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\}
for every
j\in[m]
with
v_{j}\neq v_{j+1}
to the multiset
E(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})
(we preserve multiplicities but we do not allow self loops). Then, it is not hard to see that

\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)}\left\lvert\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right\rvert,

where we define the signed weight of a multigraph
H
as
\textsc{Sw}(H)\coloneqq\prod_{e\in E(H)}(\mathbf{A}_{e}-p)
such that multiplicites of edges are respected in the product.

Now, to bound the right-hand side of the above, we employ a strategy similar to the one used in
[
24
]
for the Gaussian mixture block model. Namely, we bound the expected signed weight of a given Eulerian graph
H
with
\ell\leqslant m
edges and no self-loops by ‘contracting’ all cycles and chains in
H
to reduce
H
to a ‘core’
H^{\circ}
allowing us to treat edges inside and outside of
H^{\circ}
separately. Crucially, the core
H^{\circ}
is still a Eulerian multigraph but it now has minimum degree
4
, limiting the number of vertices it can contain to
m/2
. Moreover, conditional on the positions of vertices in
H^{\circ}
, any removed cycle or contracted chain appears independently and its contribution to
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
can be bounded using
Theorem

2.6
. The core
H^{\circ}
itself only has a limited contribution because the number of vertices in
H^{\circ}
is limited by
m/2
, which in turn limits the number of possible embeddings of a such
H^{\circ}
in
K_{n}
. On a high level, this already explains the upper bound from
Theorem

1.3
and the phase transition occurring at
d\approx n
(assuming that
p
is a constant):

•

If
d\gg n
, then the sum (
1
) is dominated by those
H
that contain as few contractable edges on chains or cycles of length
\geqslant 3
as possible. Given a fixed number
m
of edges in
H
, this property implies that
H
contains few vertices, i.e., that our sum is dominated by
dense

H
. Precisely, it is not hard to see that a Eulerian multigraph
H
with
m
edges and without chains or cycles of length
\geqslant 3
that can be contracted contains at most
1+m/2
vertices; this number is achieved exactly if
H
is the union of
m/2
cycles of length
2
arranged in a tree-like fashion. Since there are
O\left(n^{1+m/2}\right)
embeddings of a such
H
in
K_{n}
each of which roughly makes at most a constant contribution to the sum, we get that

\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}\approx\left(n\cdot n^{m/2}\right)^{1/m}=n^{1/m}\sqrt{n},

which tends to
\sqrt{n}
if
m
is sufficiently large compared to
n
. The above further suggests that there are
\approx n
eigenvalues of order
\sqrt{n}
, which is the same as we would expect in a
G\left(n,p\right)
.

•

If
d\ll n
, then the opposite occurs and the contribution of a single
H
to the sum becomes larger the more contractable chains and cycles it contains. This means that the sum is essentially dominated by sparse
H
, i.e., those which contain the same number of vertices and edges. Hence, the largest contribution to the sum in (
1
) is achieved simply by cycles with
m
edges.
There are
\leqslant n^{m}
ways of embedding a such cycle into
K_{n}
each of which has a contribution of
\approx(1/\sqrt{d})^{m-2}
to the sum such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}\approx\left(n^{m}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m-2}\right)^{1/m}=d^{1/m}\left(\frac{n}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

which is
n^{o(1)}(n/\sqrt{d})
when letting
m
tend to
\infty
. This further suggests that now there are
\approx d
large eigenvalues each of order
n/\sqrt{d}
.

We remark that the factor of
n^{o(1)}
in our upper bound comes from the fact that our expansions in their current form only allow us to handle the case of constant but arbitrarily large
m
. In order to improve this error to a polylogarithmic factor, we would need
m=\Omega(n)
. While we stronly believe our techniques to be adaptable to the regime
m=\Theta(n)
as well, we decided to limit our attention to constant
m
here for the sake of technical simplicity.

However, we are able to generalize the described results to the case of general
p
, namely to any
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
.
In this case, a similar phenomenon occurs but we have to be more careful with the contribution of edges in the core
H^{\circ}
and edges on degenerate cycles (i.e. cycles with only two edges). Our bounds on
\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}
further allow us to make a statement about the number of large eigenvalues in a
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
since we are able to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]\leqslant\begin{cases}\widetilde{O}\left(d\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}\right)&\text{if }d\ll np\log^{2}(n)\\
\widetilde{O}\left(n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)&\text{if }d\gg np\log^{2}(n)\end{cases}

(3)

for every fixed even
m
. If there were
\Omega(d^{1+\varepsilon})
eigenvalues of order
\Omega(np/\sqrt{d})
while
d\ll np
, this would contradict the above.

While our proof is similar to the idea used in
[
24
]
, we need to be significantly more careful in estimating the contribution of different Eulerian multigraphs
H
to our sum (
1
) in order to arrive at the statement in (
3
). In
[
24
]
a weaker bound suffices since the authors are only interested in an upper bound on the spectral norm of the considered matrix. To prove our bounds on the number of large eigenvalues, we require the stronger statement from (
3
), which we achieve by proving that the sum in (
1
) is indeed dominated by cycles of length
m
while all other Eulerian multigraphs
H
only contribute a lower-order term. To do this, we need a bound on the contribution of non-contracted edges which is stronger than the one used in
[
24
]
, and we explicitly distinguish between those
H
which have a trivial and non-trivial core, respectively, where a core is trivial if it only consists of a single, isolated vertex.

2.7
Application to Other Metric Spaces

We note that our technique is not limited to the torus, but can be applied to more general metric spaces for which the cumulants associated to analogous random vectors have similar properties as in our case. For example, they almost instantly carry over to other product spaces, provided only that the CGFs in each dimension continue to meet Cramer’s condition
Definition

2.5
) and the space remains homogeneous. For example, this is the case when taking the cartesian product of
d
spheres
\mathbb{S}^{k}
(each of constant dimension
k
) instead of the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, which yields the torus.

However, the presented ideas are not limited to such product spaces and we expect our techniques to carry over more generally if (1) the space is homogeneous, and (2) the magnitude of cumulants decays exponentially in their order
s
while (3) the total cumulant generating function (the CGF after taking the sum over all dimensions) satisfies an analog of Cramér’s condition (cf.
Definition

2.5
) in the sense that its absolute value decays exponentially in
d
for all
\mathbf{t}
with
\|\mathbf{t}\|\geqslant\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
.
For example, we expect these conditions to be met when replacing the torus by a
d
-dimensional sphere equipped with
L_{2}
norm, and thus believe that our method can be used to recover the known results for spherical RGGs.

Moreover, we expect our techniques to give meaningful results even for non-homogeneous spaces like a hypercube, Gaussian space or when using non-uniform distributions on the torus or the sphere. In such cases, as mentioned earlier, convergence to Erdős–Rényi in the high-dimensional limit is not always given, so we cannot always expect analogous results as presented here for the torus. This is mathematically reflected in the fact that certain mixed cumulants of low order are no longer zero and will therefore introduce additional error terms. However, we expect that suitable modifications of our expansions that explicitly take such additional terms into account can be used to study the properties of RGGs on such spaces as well. In particular, we would expect that we can recover known results for Gaussian RGGs since these only “mildly” violate homogeneity, as was already noted in
[
3
]
.

3
Preliminaries

We start with some preliminaries on characteristic functions, cumulants and Taylor expansions.

3.1
Characteristic Functions, Cumulants, Vector Calculus

For two distributions
\nu,\mu
, we denote their total variation distance by
\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu,\mu\right)
. We write vectors as bold, lower-case letters. For a column vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we denote by
\mathbf{z}(i)
its
i
-th entry, and by
\mathbf{z}^{\mathsf{T}}
its transpose, so that
\mathbf{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{y}
denotes the inner product. We denote the set of
k
–tuples by
\mathbb{N}^{\times k}
and – given a
k
–tuple
s=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})
– we define
|s|=s_{1}+s_{2}+\ldots+s_{k}
and
s!=s_{1}!s_{2}!\cdots s_{k}!
.
Using these conventions, we define the characteristic function (CF) of a random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as

\displaystyle C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(i(\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{z})\right)\right]\text{ for any }\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

where
i=\sqrt{-1}
. Taking the logarithm, we obtain the cumulant generating function (CGF, also known as the characteristic exponent) that can be expanded as a power series to yield the expression

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{z}}((t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})^{\mathsf{T}})=\log\left(C_{\mathbf{z}}((t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})^{\mathsf{T}})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})\frac{(it_{1})^{s_{1}}(it_{2})^{s_{2}}\cdots(it_{k})^{s_{k}}}{s_{1}!s_{2}!\cdots s_{k}!}.

(4)

where the sum runs over all
k
–tuples and the
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})
are the coefficients of the power series expansion of
K_{\mathbf{z}}
, which we call
cumulants
. The cumulants
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})
for which
s
contains only exactly one non-zero entry are called
pure
, all other cumulants are
mixed
. The
order
of
\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})
is
|s|
. Note further that we can express cumulants as an
s
-th order partial derivative of
K_{\mathbf{z}}
as

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=(-i)^{|s|}\left.\frac{\partial^{|s|}}{\partial t_{1}^{s_{1}}\partial t_{2}^{s_{2}}\cdots\partial t_{k}^{s_{k}}}\log\left(C_{\mathbf{z}}((t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})^{\mathsf{T}})\right)\right|_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{k}=0}.

For any collection of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{r}
, it is convenient to define the
ordinary joint cumulant

\displaystyle\kappa(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{r})=(-i)^{r}\left.\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial t_{1}\partial t_{2}\cdots\partial t_{k}}\log\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(i\sum_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}X_{j}\right)\right]\right)\right|_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{k}=0}

which then allows us to equivalently define

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})=\kappa(\underbrace{\mathbf{z}(1),\ldots,\mathbf{z}(1)}_{s_{1}\text{ times}},\underbrace{\mathbf{z}(2),\ldots,\mathbf{z}(2)}_{s_{2}\text{ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{\mathbf{z}(k),\ldots,\mathbf{z}(k)}_{s_{k}\text{ times}}).

That is, we can define the joint cumulants of
\mathbf{z}
by repeating the
i
-th entry of
\mathbf{z}
for
s_{i}
times as an argument of
\kappa
. In particular, this definition allows us to relate cumulants to mixed moments by invoking a Möbius inversion over the partition lattice

\displaystyle\kappa(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{k})=\sum_{\pi}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in B}X_{j}\right]

(5)

where the sum goes over all partitions
\pi
of
[k]=\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
|\pi|
denotes the number of blocks of said partition. We refer the interested reader to
(
29
, Chapter 3)
for more details and a rigorous proof of the above. Cumulants further have many nice properties, the most important of which we capture in the following propositions.

Proposition 3.1

(Additivity of cumulants)
.

Consider a random vector
\mathbf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{z}_{i}
where the
\mathbf{z}_{i}
are i.i.d. Then for all
s\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z}_{i}).

Proposition 3.2

(Homogeneity of cumulants)
.

Consider a random vector
\mathbf{z}
. Then for all
s\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}
and all
c\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}(c\cdot\mathbf{z})=c^{|s|}\kappa_{s}(\mathbf{z}).

Both properties are standard and can easily be derived from the definition of the CGF above. However, working with cumulants in this form can be tedious. To remedy this we also use a ‘vectorized’ version of (
4
) which relies on the Kronecker product
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}
between two vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, defined as the
k^{2}
dimensional vector

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{x}(1)\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{x}(2)\mathbf{y}\\
\cdots\\
\mathbf{x}(k)\mathbf{y}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{2}}.

We further denote by
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes j}
the
j
-times repeated Kronecker product with itself, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes j}=\underbrace{\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{x}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{x}}_{j\text{ times}}.

Then we can put all cumulants of order
j
into a vector
\bm{\kappa}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{j}}
and express
K_{\mathbf{z}}
as
5
5

5

When putting cumulants of order
j
into a vector, we further have to multiply them by a suitable constant to compensate the quotient of
j!
and
s_{1}!s_{2}!\cdots s_{k}!
for
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
with
|s|=j
.

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\bm{\kappa}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}.

Occasionally, we shall refer to
K_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})
as
K(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{z})
instead, for the sake of easier readability. These two notations refer to the same function. We further use the following version of Taylor’s theorem.

Theorem 3.3

(Proposition 6 in
[
23
]
)
.

For
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
J
times continuously differentiable, we have

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=r_{J}(\mathbf{x})+\sum_{j=0}^{J}\frac{1}{j!}f^{(j)}(\mathbf{0})^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes j}

where

r_{J}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{J!}\left(f^{(J)}(c\mathbf{x})-f^{(J)}(\mathbf{0})\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes J}

and
c
is some value in
[0,1]
that can depend on
\mathbf{x}
. Here,
f^{(j)}
denotes the ‘
j
-th derivative of
f
’, i.e., the vector containing all order
j
partial derivatives of
f
, or alternatively
f^{(j)}=\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}f(x).

It is also convenient to note the following proposition about the Kronecker product.

Observation 3.4
.

For every
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we have
\|(i\mathbf{t})^{\otimes j}\|\leqslant(\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t})^{\frac{j}{2}}
.

Another important theorem we rely on is the inversion theorem, which states that given the CF of a random vector with continuous density we can reverse the Fourier transform to recover its density.

Theorem 3.5

(Inversion Theorem,
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a random vector with integrable characteristic function, i.e., assume that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}|C_{\mathbf{z}}|(\mathbf{t})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}<\infty.

Then the density
f
of
\mathbf{z}
exists and

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}C_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{t})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}}\exp\left(K(\mathbf{t})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t}.

(6)

Furthermore, we define Cramér’s condition, which intuitively requires that the absolute value of the CF of a random vector is bounded away from
1
for all
\mathbf{t}
with
\|\mathbf{t}\|\geqslant\delta
, for all
\delta>0
