that subsequently allows us to integrate only over a small region around the origin. We define the interval
D=[-\log(n),\hat{\tau}]
and note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\in D)\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)<-\log(n))\right)\cap\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\in D)\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)<-\log(n))\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\in D)\right)+kn^{-\omega(1)}

by the tail bound from
Lemma

3.7
. To bound the remaining probability, we apply
Theorem

2.4
and choose the parameters
a_{\textup{pure}},a_{\textup{mix}},b_{\textup{pure}},b_{\textup{mix}}
all large enough such that our approximation error satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|f(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle=O\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}},|\kappa|\right\}^{a_{\textup{mix}}+1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{(k-2)(a_{\textup{mix}}+1)}+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\min\{b_{\textup{pure}}-2,b_{\textup{mix}}-2,a_{\textup{pure}}+1\}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{p^{k}}{d^{k}}\right),

which is possible since all bases of the above exponentials are at most
n^{-\varepsilon}
and since
p\geqslant n^{-O\left(1\right)}
. Now, after applying our main theorem, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{z}(i)\in D)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{D^{k}}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}+O\left(\frac{p^{k}\log^{k}(n)}{d^{k}}\right)

since we integrate over a set of volume
O\left(\log^{k}(n)\right)
while having an error of
O\left(p^{k}/d^{k}\right)
. It remains to bound the above integral. Using the expression for
\tilde{f}
from
Theorem

2.4
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{D^{k}}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{k}}f_{\textup{ind}}(\mathbf{x})\textup{d}\mathbf{x}+(-1)^{k}\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\int_{D^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}(j))\text{d}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{j=4}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\int_{D^{k}}\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\int_{D}f_{\textup{ind}}(\mathbf{x})\textup{d}x\right)^{k}+|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\left(\int_{D}\phi^{(1)}(x)\text{d}x\right)^{k}+\sum_{j=4}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\int_{D^{k}}\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}.

Applying
Claim

5.2
,
Claim

5.3
and
Claim

5.4
, we get that

\displaystyle\int_{D^{k}}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\left(p+O\left(\frac{p^{k}}{d^{k}}\right)\right)^{k}+Cp^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+O\left(p^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+p^{k}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=p^{k}+O\left(p^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+p^{k}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\right).

In total, this means that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{k}+O\left(p^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+p^{k}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\right)+O\left(\frac{p^{k}}{d^{k}}\right)+O\left(\frac{p^{k}\log^{k}(n)}{d^{k}}\right)+kn^{-\omega(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{k}+O\left(p^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+p^{k}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\right)

as desired for the case of a chain. In case of a cycle, we simply note that
|\kappa|=O\left(1\right)
and the result follows.
∎

It only remains to prove
Claim

5.2
,
Claim

5.3
, and
Claim

5.4
.

Proof of
Claim

5.2
.

Factorizing and using that all components of
\mathbf{z}
marginally have the same distribution yields

\displaystyle\int_{D^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\mathbf{z}(j)}(\mathbf{x}(j))\textup{d}\mathbf{x}\leqslant\left(\int_{D}f_{\mathbf{z}(1)}(x)\textup{d}x\right)^{k}=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}(i)\in D\right)^{k}\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau}\right)^{k}.

Now, we get by
Lemma

3.6
and
Corollary

3.16
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}(1)\leqslant\hat{\tau}\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{r}(1)\leqslant\hat{\tau}\right)+O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{\eta-1}}\right)=p+O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{\eta-1}}\right)

and the proof is finished.
∎

Proof of
Claim

5.3
.

Factorizing yields that

\displaystyle|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\int_{D^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}(j))\text{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\left(\int_{D}\phi^{(1)}(x)\text{d}x\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\phi(\hat{\tau})^{k}\leqslant Cp^{k}|\kappa|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}

where we used
Corollary

3.17
.
∎

Proof of
Claim

5.4
.

We note that for any
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}\\
|s|=j\end{subarray}}\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}\left|\frac{\partial^{|s|}}{\partial^{s_{1}}x_{1}\cdots\partial^{s_{k}}x_{k}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right|

(17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}\\
|s|=j\end{subarray}}\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}|P_{s}(\mathbf{x})|\phi(\mathbf{x})

(18)

where the
P_{s}
are a collection of polynomials each of which is a product of Hermite polynomials whose order sums up to
j
(i.e. each
P_{s}
is of order
j
). Since each
\mathbf{x}\in D^{k}
fulfills
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\log(n)
by
Corollary

3.16
, we get that
|P_{s}(\mathbf{x})|=O\left(\log^{j}(n)\right)
for
\mathbf{x}\in D^{k}
. Thus, when integrating, we get

\displaystyle\int_{D^{k}}\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}\leqslant O\left(\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}\log^{j}(n)\right)\int_{D^{k}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}=O\left(p^{k}\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}\log^{j}(n)\right)

since

\displaystyle\int_{D^{k}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}=\left(\int_{D}\phi(x)\text{d}x\right)^{k}\leqslant\Phi(\hat{\tau})^{k}\leqslant Cp^{k}

by
Corollary

3.17
. To bound
\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}\log^{j}(n)
, we use our bound from
Theorem

2.4
to obtain

\displaystyle\log^{k+j}(n)\|\bm{\alpha}_{k+j}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\kappa|\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j}+\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}\left(\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j}+\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\kappa|\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-2}+\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k-1}

where used that
\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j}\leqslant 1
since
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
. We remark that we are intentionally loose with the log factors here for the sake of simplicity.
∎

6
Application 1: Tight Testing Thresholds via Signed Triangles

In this section, we prove that signed triangles provide a test that distinguishes a RGG from a
G(n,p)
if
d=\tilde{o}(n^{3}p^{3})
. This is a significant improvement over the previous best algorithmic upper bound for detecting geometry in a
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
proven by Bangachev and Bresler
(
2
, Theorem 1.11)
which asserts only that
\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)=1-o(1)
if
d=\tilde{o}(np)
. The argument in
[
2
]
does not rely on signed triangles but instead uses an entropy-based argument built upon an explicit
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with specific properties, which has the drawback of not giving a computationally efficient test. We analyze the signed triangle count

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\coloneqq\sum_{i<j<k\in[n]}\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\text{ where }\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\coloneqq(\mathbf{G}_{ij}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{ik}-p)(\mathbf{G}_{jk}-p)

yielding a more powerful – but still efficient – algorithm for detecting geometry. The result, stated in the following theorem, matches the information-theoretic lower bound in case
p=\Omega(1)
.

Theorem
1.1

(Explicitly stated)
.

For any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
and any
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
for
\gamma>0
arbitrarily small, the number of signed triangles distinguishes
\mathbf{G}_{1}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}\sim G\left(n,p\right)
with probability
1-o(1)
whenever
d=o(n^{3}p^{3})
, and fails at doing so whenever
d=\omega(n^{3}p^{3})
.
Precisely,

\displaystyle\left|\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]-\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]\right|-\max\left\{\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]},\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\omega(1)&\text{if }d=o(n^{3}p^{3})\\
o(1)&\text{if }d=\tilde{\omega}(n^{3}p^{3}).\end{cases}

To prove
Theorem

1.1
, we bound the expected signed triangle count from below, and its variance from above, and the result follows by Chebyshev’s inequality.
Bounding the expectation is done using
Theorem

2.4
; the main challenge in contrast to the proof of
Theorem

2.6
is having to control the magnitude and the sign of
\kappa
to obtain a lower bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]
. In fact, we must prove that
\kappa
is a negative constant for all
q\geqslant 1
such that the main correction term
(-1)^{k}\kappa/\sqrt{d}
from
Theorem

2.4
is positive and the expectation of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})
becomes larger than in case of a
G\left(n,p\right)
. To do this we rely on the following lemmas.

Lemma 6.2
.

For a cycle
C_{k}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
of length
k
with edges
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k}
where
e_{j}=\{v_{j},v_{(j+1)\text{ }\text{mod }k}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}=(\zeta\sigma)^{-k}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\gamma(e_{j})\right]\text{ where }\gamma(\{u,v\})\coloneqq|x_{u}-x_{v}|_{C}^{q}-\mathbb{E}\left[|x_{u}-x_{v}|_{C}^{q}\right]

and where
x_{v_{j}}
is an independent sample from
\text{Unif}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
for each
v_{j}
.

See
2.7

The proof of the above statements is deferred to
Section

A.3
because of its high technical complexity. On a high level,
Lemma

2.7
relies on splitting the expected value
\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]\right]
where
y
is the distance associated to the endpoints of
e_{1}
and then employing a monotonicity argument to show that
\gamma(e_{1})
is increasing in
y
and that
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]
is decreasing in
y
. The latter argument is based on carefully controlling the derivative of
\mathbb{E}[\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\mid y]
using the Leibnitz integral rule and suitable substitutions.

6.1
The Expected Number of Signed Triangles

In this section, we prove a lower bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]
. Since
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=\binom{n}{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(C_{3})\right]
where
C_{3}
is an arbitrary but fixed triangle in
\mathbf{G}
, the following lemma, based on
Theorem

2.4
, suffices.

Lemma 6.3
.

Consider a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
for any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
and fix a triangle
C_{3}
with edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
. Then, there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\frac{1}{n^{c}}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
and all
n^{\gamma}\leqslant d\leqslant n^{O\left(1\right)}
(where
\gamma>0
is an arbitrarily small constant),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{\textsc{Sw}}(C_{3})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathds{1}(e_{1})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{2})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{3})-p)\right]\geqslant\frac{Cp^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}.

Proof.

First note that by
Lemma

5.1
, we have that
\mathrm{\textsc{Sw}}(C_{3})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}(e_{1})\mathds{1}(e_{2})\mathds{1}(e_{3})\right]-p^{3},
i.e., the signed weight of a triangle is simply the probability that a fixed set of vertices is a triangle minus
p^{3}
. Thus we are left with bounding
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}(e_{1})\mathds{1}(e_{2})\mathds{1}(e_{3})\right]
. To this end, we apply the technique described in
Section

4
. We note that by
Lemma

3.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}(e_{1})\mathds{1}(e_{2})\mathds{1}(e_{3})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}(\mathbf{r}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)-O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{\eta-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)-O\left(\frac{p^{3}}{d}\right),

since
|\tilde{\tau}|\leqslant\log(n)
by
Proposition

3.14
and since we can choose
\eta
to be
\geqslant 1+\varepsilon/\gamma
such that
\frac{\log(n)}{d^{\eta-1}}\leqslant\frac{p^{3}}{d}
, as
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
and
p\geqslant n^{-O\left(1\right)}
. Now, we use our approximation
\tilde{f}
to the density of
\mathbf{z}
from
Theorem

2.4
for which we set the parameters
a_{\textup{pure}},a_{\textup{mix}},b_{\textup{pure}},b_{\textup{mix}}
to be sufficiently large constants such that the approximation error satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|f(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle=O\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}},|\kappa|\right\}^{a_{\textup{mix}}+1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{(k-2)(a_{\textup{mix}}+1)}+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\min\{b_{\textup{pure}}-2,b_{\textup{mix}}-2,a_{\textup{pure}}+1\}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{p^{3}}{d}\right)

which again is possible since
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
. Now we can define the interval
D=[-\log(n),\tilde{\tau}]
and bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}(\mathbf{z}(i)\leqslant\hat{\tau})\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\int_{D^{3}}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}-O\left(\frac{p^{3}\log(n)^{3}}{d}\right)

where the
O\left(p^{3}\log(n)^{3}/d\right)
term comes from the fact that we have an error of
O\left(p^{3}/d\right)
and integrate over a set of volume
O\left(\log^{3}(n)\right)
. Now, noting that
\kappa=c\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]/\sqrt{d}
for some constant
c>0
by
Lemma

6.2
, using our expression for
\tilde{f}
and factorizing the integrals, we get

\displaystyle\int_{D^{3}}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{3}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{3}f_{\mathbf{z}(j)}(\mathbf{x}(j))\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}-\frac{c\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]}{\sqrt{d}}\int_{D^{3}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{3}\phi^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}(j))\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}+g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}(1)\in D\right)\right)^{3}-\frac{c\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]}{\sqrt{d}}\left(\int_{D}\phi^{(1)}(x)\text{d}x\right)^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\underbrace{\sum_{j=4}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\int_{D^{3}}\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right)\text{d}\mathbf{x}}_{\eqqcolon g(\mathbf{x})}.

Choosing
\eta
sufficiently large, we get from
Lemma

3.6
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}(1)\in D\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant p-O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{\eta-1}}\right)\geqslant p-O\left(\frac{p\log(n)}{d}\right)=p\left(1-o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\right)

and further

\displaystyle\int_{D}\phi^{(1)}(x)\text{d}x=\phi(\hat{\tau})-\underbrace{\phi(-\log(n))}_{=n^{-\omega(1)}}\geqslant Cp

where we used
Corollary

3.17
. It remains to bound the influence of the higher order correction terms captured in
g(\mathbf{x})
.
To this end,
we recall the expansion of
\bm{\alpha}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{\nabla}^{\otimes j}\phi(\mathbf{x}))
in (
17
) into a sum over polynomials
P_{s}
of degree
s

that can be represented as a product of Hermite polynomials in the individual
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
Because
|\hat{\tau}|\leqslant\log(n)
by

Corollary

3.16
, we get that
|P_{s}(\mathbf{x})|=O\left(\log^{s}(n)\right)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in D^{3}
, so using the bound on
\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}
from
Theorem

2.4
and
|\kappa|=O\left(1\right)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\bm{\alpha}_{j}\|_{\infty}|P_{s}(\mathbf{x})|\leqslant\frac{C}{\sqrt{d}}\left(\log(n)d^{-\frac{1}{6}}\right)^{j}\text{ and thus }g(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\frac{C}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{j=4}^{s_{1}b_{1}+s_{2}b_{2}}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j}\int_{D^{3}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}.

We further obtain

\displaystyle\int_{D^{3}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}=\left(\int_{D}\phi(\mathbf{x})\text{d}\mathbf{x}\right)^{3}\leqslant\Phi(\hat{\tau})^{3}\leqslant Cp^{3}

since
\Phi(\hat{\tau})\leqslant(1+o(1))p
if
p=o(1)
by
Corollary

3.17
and
\Phi(\hat{\tau})=\Theta(1)
if
p=\Omega(1)
since in this case
\hat{\tau}=\Omega(1)
by
Corollary

3.16
. Combining this yields

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\frac{Cp^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{j=4}^{a_{\textup{pure}}b_{\textup{pure}}+a_{\textup{mix}}b_{\textup{mix}}}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{1/6}}\right)^{j}=o\left(\frac{p^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

since
d^{1/6}=\omega(\log(n))
. Using this bound for
g(\mathbf{x})
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}(e_{1})\mathds{1}(e_{2})\mathds{1}(e_{3})\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant p^{3}\left(1-o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\right)^{3}-\frac{Cp^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]+o\left(\frac{p^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)-O\left(\frac{p^{3}}{d}\right)-O\left(\frac{p^{3}\log(n)^{3}}{d}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant p^{3}-\frac{Cp^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]+o\left(\frac{p^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

Finally, using that
\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})\right]
is a negative constant by
Lemma

2.7
we conclude the proof.
∎

6.2
Bounding the Variance

Lemma 6.4
.

Consider
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
for any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
. Then, for all
\frac{1}{n^{c}}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
and all
n^{\gamma}\leqslant d\leqslant n^{O\left(1\right)}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=O\left(p^{3}n^{3}+\frac{p^{3}n^{3}\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{n^{4}p^{5}\log^{2}(n)}{d}+\frac{n^{4}p^{6}\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

Proof.

We proceed as in
(
25
, Appendix A)
and define

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}\coloneqq\mathbf{T}_{ijk}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}_{ijk}\right]\text{ and }\overline{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{G})\coloneqq\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=\sum_{i<j<k\in[n]}\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}.

Now expanding the variance like in
(
25
, Appendix A)
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{G})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j<k\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}^{2}\right]+2\sum_{i<j<k<\ell\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[3\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{jk\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}^{2}\right]+6\binom{n}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{jk\ell}\right],

where we used that
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{\ell rs}\right]=0
if at most two of the
i,j,k,\ell,r,s
are the same, by independence of edges. Now, following the same calculation as in
(
25
, Appendix A)
and denoting by
\varrho=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{G}_{12}\mathbf{G}_{13}=1\mid\mathbf{G}_{23}=1\right)-1
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}^{2}]=O\left((1+\varrho)p^{3}\right)\text{ and }\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{ijk}\overline{\mathbf{T}}_{jk\ell}\right]=O\left(p^{5}\varrho^{2}+p^{6}\varrho\right).

Noting that by our
Theorem

2.6
, we get

\displaystyle\varrho=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{G}_{12}\mathbf{G}_{13}=1\mid\mathbf{G}_{23}=1\right)-1=\frac{1}{p^{3}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{G}_{12}\mathbf{G}_{13}\mathbf{G}_{23}=1\right)-1=O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

we can conclude the proof.
∎

With this, we prove
Theorem

1.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Noting that
\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=0
and
\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]=\binom{n}{3}p^{3}(1-p)^{3}
, and using
Lemma

6.3
as well as
Lemma

6.4
,

\displaystyle\left|\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]-\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]\right|=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{3}p^{3}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

while

\displaystyle\max\left\{\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]},\sqrt{\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim G\left(n,p\right)}{\mathbf{Var}}\left[\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{G})\right]}\right\}=O\left(\sqrt{np}^{3}\right).

The result follows.
∎

7
Application 2: Improved Information-Theoretic Lower Bounds for all Densities

Another application of
Theorem

2.6
is an improved algorithmic lower bound that works for all densities
p
. This improves upon the previous best lower bound from
(
2
, Theorem 1.10)
that only holds in the case of
p=1/2
. The bound constructed here works for all fixed
q\geqslant 1
and is (up to logarithmic factors) as strong as the current best information-theoretic lower bound for spherical RGGs from
[
26
]
in the case of general
p
.
6
6

6

In case of
p=c/n
for a constant
c
,
[
26
]
give a stronger bound based on a specialized argument exploiting the locally-tree-like properties of sparse RGGs. However, this analysis breaks down in the case of general
p
as the locally-tree-like properties are lost. In this case, their lower bounds are just as strong as ours.
Formally, we prove the following.
See
1.2

The proof is based on a technique introduced by Liu and Rácz
[
28
]
, which – after employing Pinsker’s inequality – relates the total variation distance between
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
G\left(n,p\right)
to a sum over the moments of the self-convolution of two specific edge indicator random variables.
It is not hard to see that this bound can be expanded as a weighted sum over the expected signed weight of complete bipartite graphs with
k
vertices on the left, and
2
vertices on the right side of the bipartition, denoted by
K_{2,k}
. We then rely on the following bound on the expected signed weight of a
K_{2,k}
based on another application of
Theorem

2.6
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Assume that
d=\omega(n\log(n))
.
Then, for any fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
, there is a constant
C>0
such that for any
\frac{1}{n^{c}}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
and all
2\leqslant k\leqslant n
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]^{1/k}\leqslant C\log(d)\log^{2}(n)p^{2}\sqrt{\frac{k}{d}}.

In contrast to the other applications of
Theorem

2.6
, here the bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]
must be valid for
k
up to the same order of magnitude as
n
(other applications rely on the fact that the graphs over which we compute the expected signed weight are small).

7
7

7

Technically, the random variable
\textsc{Sw}(H)
is only defined for labeled graphs
H
. However, as its law (and hence its expectation) is invariant under graph isomorphism, we can make sense of the expression
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]
by fixing an arbitrary vertex labeling from
[n]
.

However, due to the fact that a
K_{2,k}
, conditioned on the position of the two vertices on the right, is a union of
k
chains of length
2
,
Theorem

2.6
is applicable in this setting as well, after taking care of some technical precautions.
Before proving
Proposition

7.1
, we show how it helps us in proving
Theorem

1.2
combined with the bound of Liu and Rácz. Afterwards (in
Section

7.2
), we prove
Proposition

7.1
.

7.1
Using the Bound of Liu and Rácz

To illustrate why
Proposition

7.1
leads to an improved algorithmic lower bound, we refer to the work of Liu and Racz, who showed in
(
28
, Section 3)
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(\underset{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]\right),

\displaystyle\text{ with }\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}}[(\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})-p)(\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})-p)],

where
\mathds{1}_{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
denotes the indicator random variable for the event that
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\|_{q}\leqslant\tau
and that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\sim\textup{Unif}\left(\left[-1/2,1/2\right]\right)^{d}
, independently.
Expanding the above expectation, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left[\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right],

and hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(1+\sum_{j=2}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right]\right).

It is very important that the inner sum above starts at
j=2
, which is true since
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,1})\right]
is clearly zero as the edges are independent in this case.
With this, and the inequality
\log(1+x)\leqslant x
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(1+\sum_{j=2}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{j=2}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant n\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,j})\right].

Now, with
Proposition

7.1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right),G\left(n,p\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant n\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\left(\frac{Cp^{2}\log(d)\log^{2}(n)\sqrt{j}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant n\sum_{j=2}^{n}\left(\frac{ne}{j}\right)^{j}\frac{1}{{p^{j}(1-p)^{j}}}\left(\frac{Cp^{2}\log(d)\log^{2}(n)j}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle=n\sum_{j=2}^{n}\left(Ce\frac{np\log(d)\log^{2}(n)}{(1-p)\sqrt{d}}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant n\left(Ce\frac{np\log(d)\log^{2}(n)}{(1-p)\sqrt{d}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\frac{C^{\prime}n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}(d)\log^{4}(n)}{d}

where in the penultimate step we used that the sum is geometric if
d=\omega(\log^{2}(n)n^{2}p^{2})
. The whole expression becomes
o(1)
if
d=\omega(\log^{6}(n)n^{3}p^{2})=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3}p^{2})
as desired. It thus only remains to prove
Proposition

7.1
.

7.2
Proving
Proposition

7.1

Proof of
Proposition

7.1
.

For a
K_{2,k}
with vertices
v_{1}
on one side and vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
on the other, we denote the edge from
v_{i}
to
u_{j}
by
e_{ij}
. If we denote the position of
v_{1},v_{2}
by
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}
, respectively, and the position of
u_{1}
by
\mathbf{y}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}(\mathds{1}(e_{1j})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{2j})-p)\right]=\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\mathds{1}(e_{11})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{21})-p)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\right]^{k}\right].

The inner expectation is now essentially the signed weight of a chain of length
k=2
with endpoints
v_{1},v_{2}
raised to the power of
k
, so we naturally wish to apply our bound from
Theorem

2.6
. This bound heavily depends on
|\kappa|
, which in this case is essentially equal to the covariance between the rescaled distances associated to
e_{11}
and
e_{12}
. However, this covariance depends on the positioning of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. We shall therefore split the above expectation into two parts where the first part that represents the case in which
v_{1},v_{2}
are positioned such that
|\kappa|
is small and the second part represents the remaining case. To this end, we define
\mathcal{E}
to be the event that a chain with endpoints
v_{1},v_{2}
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-good (cf.
Definition

4.1
) for some constant
\alpha>0
and

\displaystyle\beta\leqslant C^{\prime}\log(d/p^{2})\sqrt{k/d}

where
C^{\prime}>0
is a suitable constant to be fixed later. We note that

\displaystyle\kappa=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\kappa_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{z}_{i})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{z}_{i}(1)\mathbf{z}_{i}(2)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\right]=\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{r}_{i}(1)\mathbf{r}_{i}(2)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\right]

by using the decomposition from (
8
) in
Section

3.2.1
and expanding the expectation. We note that
\zeta^{2}d\kappa
is a random variable in which the randomness comes solely from
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}
. Furthermore, we have that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\zeta^{2}d\kappa\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{r}_{i}(1)\mathbf{r}_{i}(2)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\right]\right]=0,

so
\zeta^{2}d\kappa
is a mean zero random variable and further a sum of
d
i.i.d. random variables, each of which is bounded almost surely by some constant
M
. Hence, applying the Bernstein bound from
Theorem

3.13
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\kappa|\geqslant t\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}}{2M^{2}d+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(\max\left\{\frac{-t^{2}}{4M^{2}d},\frac{-t}{\frac{4}{3}M}\right\}\right)

so setting
t=C^{\prime}\log(d/p^{2})\sqrt{kd}
for a suitable constant
C^{\prime}>0
yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\kappa|\geqslant t\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left(\max\left\{\frac{-C^{\prime 2}\log^{2}(d/p^{2})k}{4M^{2}},\frac{-C^{\prime}\log(d/p^{2})\sqrt{kd}}{\frac{4}{3}M}\right\}\right)\leqslant\left(\frac{p^{2}}{d}\right)^{2k}

since we assume that
\omega(n\log(n))
and
k\leqslant n
so
\sqrt{kd}\geqslant k
.
This shows that
|\kappa|\leqslant\beta
with probability
1-\left(\frac{p^{2}}{d}\right)^{2k}
. Furthermore, we get from
Lemma

4.3
that with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(d))
, there is a constant
\alpha>0
such that at least
\alpha d
out of
d
dimensions are such that Cramér’s condition is met. Both statements together imply that there is a constant
C^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})\leqslant\left(\frac{p^{2}}{d}\right)^{2k}+e^{-C^{\prime\prime}d}\leqslant 2\left(\frac{p^{2}}{d}\right)^{2k}

because
d=\omega(n\log(n))
and
k\leqslant n
. Now, denoting the law of
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}
conditional on
\mathcal{E}
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
, respectively, by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\mathds{1}(e_{11})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{21})-p)\right]^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathcal{E})}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\mathds{1}(e_{11})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{21})-p)\right]^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\hskip 21.33955pt\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\mathds{1}(e_{11})-p)(\mathds{1}(e_{21})-p)\right]^{k}\right]+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}).

Applying
Theorem

2.6
, yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(K_{2,k})\right]\leqslant\left(C^{\prime\prime}p^{2}\left(\log(d/p^{2})\sqrt{\frac{k}{d}}+\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\right)^{k}+2\left(\frac{p^{2}}{d}\right)^{2k}\leqslant\left(C\log(d)\log^{2}(n)p^{2}\sqrt{\frac{k}{d}}\right)^{k}

as desired.
∎

8
Application 3: Eigenvalues and the Spectral Threshold

We use our bound on Fourier coefficients of cycles and chains to prove tight bounds on the spectral gap of a typical
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
. Our main result, which we prove in this section, is as follows.
See
1.3

Proof.

The statement follows from the upper bound from
Lemma

8.1
and the lower bound from
Lemma

8.17
in case
d\ll np
, proved in the following subsections. For
d=\Omega(np)
, the lower bound of
C\sqrt{np}
on
\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})
follows from the generalized Alon–Boppana bound from
(
22
, Theorem 8)
using
r=1
.
∎

8.1
Upper Bound via the Trace Method

In this section, we prove the following upper bound on the second largest eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
of a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Consider a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
for any
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
. With probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}\leqslant n^{o(1)}\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}

where
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})
are the eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
of a
\mathbf{G}
. Moreover, if
d\ll np^{1+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, the number of eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A})
with
|\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\geqslant\frac{np}{a\sqrt{d}}
is at most
\widetilde{O}(da^{\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+4})
.

The upper bound of
Theorem

1.3
and
Theorem

1.4
is proved via the trace method, which we briefly sketch here. As the name suggests, it can be used to prove bounds on the largest eigenvalue of a matrix via its trace. Since we are interested in the second largest eigenvalue, we consider the centered adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}_{p}=\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
where
\mathds{1}
is the
n
-dimensional all-ones vector. This subtracts the eigenspace associated to the largest eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}
(which is
\mathds{1}
). To bound the largest eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we first recall the well-known relation

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right).

Considering the
m
-th power of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\text{, so }\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|^{m}\leqslant\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)

for every even
m
. By Markov’s inequality, we have that for every
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|^{m}\geqslant a\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{a}.

Choosing any
a=\omega(1)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\geqslant a^{1/m}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}\right)\leqslant 1/a=o(1)

(19)

and we are left with bounding the above expectation. Conveniently,
\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)
has a combinatorial interpretation.
Namely, it can be expressed as a sum over all directed, closed walks of length
m
on the complete graph with vertex set
[n]
, where each term in the sum corresponds to the product of the entries of
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
along the edges of the walk.
Formally, writing
\textsc{Ew}(n,m)\coloneqq\{(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in[n]^{m+1}\mid v_{1}=v_{m+1}\}
for the set of closed walks of length
m
, we have

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)=\sum_{(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\big(\mathbf{A}_{v_{j},v_{j+1}}-p\big).

(20)

We proceed by deriving an upper bound on the expectation of the right-hand side of (
20
), by representing it as signed weights of (multi-)graphs and analyzing the corresponding signed weights in the following subsection.

Bounding the signed weight of Eulerian multigraphs

We give a graphical interpretation of the right-hand side of (
20
).
To this end, we extend our definition of the signed weight of a graph to (undirected) multigraphs (in which there can be multiple edges between any pair of vertices).
Formally, given a multigraph
H
with vertex set
V(H)\subseteq[n]
and multiset of edges
E(H)
over the universe
\binom{[V(H)]}{2}
, we define the signed weight of
H
as the random variable

\textsc{Sw}(H)\coloneqq\prod_{e\in E(H)}(\mathds{1}\left(e\right)-p)=\prod_{e\in E(H)}(\mathbf{A}_{e}-p),

where the product respects multiplicities of edges in
H
, and, for any edge
e=\{v,w\}\in\binom{[n]}{2}
, we write
\mathbf{A}_{e}=\mathbf{A}_{v,w}
.
Moreover, it holds that
H_{\boldsymbol{v}}
has at most
m
edges in total.

Next, given a walk
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)
, we define
H_{\boldsymbol{v}}
to be the multigraph on the vertex set
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1}\}
(duplications of vertices are removed), where we add an edge
\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\}
for every
j\in[m]
with
v_{j}\neq v_{j+1}
to the multiset
E(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})
(we preserve multiplicities but we do not allow self loops).
Note that the resulting multigraph
H
is Eulerian, in the sense that
H
is connected, and all vertices have even degree. We proceed by observing that, for every
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1})\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)
, it holds that

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\big(\mathbf{A}_{v_{j},v_{j+1}}-p\big)\right]\right\rvert=p^{m-|E(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})|}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right]\right\rvert,

where we use that every self loop in the walk
\boldsymbol{v}
contributes an additional factor of
-p
compared to
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right]
.
Hence, recalling (
20
), we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right]\right\rvert.

(21)

To bound the right-hand side of the above inequality, we employ a strategy similar to the one used in
[
24
]
for the Gaussian mixture block model. That is, we bound the expected signed weight of a given Eulerian graph
H
with
\ell\leqslant m
edges and no self-loops by considering edges within cycles and chains and edges outside of such structures separately. Precisely, we repeatedly remove cycles from
H
and afterwards contract all chains into a single edge, which leaves us with a (multi)graph
H^{\circ}
called the
core
of
H
. The core
H^{\circ}
is still Eulerian but now has minimum degree
4
, which limits the number of vertices it can contain to
m/2
. Now, conditional on the positions of vertices in
H^{\circ}
, any removed cycle or contracted chain appears independently and its contribution to
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
can be bounded using
Theorem

2.6
. Edges in
H^{\circ}
only have a limited contribution because the number of vertices in
H^{\circ}
is limited by
m/2
, which in turn limits the number of possible embeddings of a such
H^{\circ}
in
K_{n}
. We refer the reader to
Section

2.6.3
for an intuitive explanation of our argument and proceed by making our ideas formal.

Constructing the Core
H^{\circ}
.

The following definitions formalize which chains/cycles of vertices can be contracted/removed.

Definition 8.2

(Chains)
.

Consider a Eulerian multigraph
H
with
\ell
edges. For every
k\geqslant 3
, we call a sequence
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}
of
distinct
vertices from
V(H)
a
chain
if
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}\in E(H)
for all
i\in[k-1]
, and if
\deg(v_{i})=2
for all
2\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

Definition 8.3

(Cycles)
.

Consider a Eulerian multigraph
H
with
\ell
edges. We call a sequence
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k+1}
of vertices a
cycle
if
k\geqslant 3
, if
v_{1}=v_{k+1}
, if
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
contains no duplicates, if
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}\in E(H)
for all
i\in[k-1]
, and if
\deg(v_{i})=2
for all
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
. In particular,
v_{1}
is allowed to have degree
\geqslant 2
and is referred to as the
anchor
of our cycle. Further, if
v
is a vertex which only has a single neighbor
u
(possibly there are multiple edges between
u
and
v
),
u
and
v
are a cycle of length
k=2
and
u
is the anchor. Cycles of length
k=2
are called
degenerate
.

The algorithm that transforms an
\ell
-edge Eulerian multigraph
H
into its core
H^{\circ}
works as follows. It begins by iteratively removing all cycles in
H
. That is, while there still exists a cycle in
H
, it removes all vertices on said cycle except for the anchor.
8
8

8

If there is no clear anchor, i.e., if all vertices have degree
2
, choose an arbitrary vertex on the cycle as the anchor.
After all cycles have been removed, the algorithm contracts chains: while there still exists chain in
H
, replace all intermediate vertices by a single edge between the two endpoints of the chain. The result of this procedure is the core
H^{\circ}
.

It is immediate that
H^{\circ}
is a Eulerian multigraph, which is either a single isolated vertex (which happens if all edges were removed while contracting cycles) or a multigraph which contains at least
2
vertices and has minimum degree
4
. We split the set of edges of
H^{\circ}
into two disjoint parts
E_{U}
and
E_{C}
,
where
E_{U}
is the set of
non-contracted
edges, i.e., those that are both in
H^{\circ}
and
H
, and
E_{C}
is the set of edges created by contracting a chain into a single edge. We further denote by
L
(which stands for
loops
) the set of edges of
H
on cycles that were removed in step 1 of the above algorithm. Moreover, we denote by
U
the graph induced by all non-contracted edges, i.e., the graph obtained from
H^{\circ}
after deleting all edges not in
E_{U}
and afterwards removing all isolated vertices. We can further relate the number of vertices of the core
H^{\circ}
and
H
itself using the following straightforward combinatorial claim.

Lemma 8.4
.

In the setting above, it holds that

|V(H)|=|E(H)|-|L|-|E(H^{\circ})|+|V(H^{\circ})|.

Moreover,
|V(H^{\circ})|\leqslant\max\{|E(H^{\circ})|/2,1\}
.

Furthermore, when summing over the signed weight of all multigraphs
H
corresponding to Eulerian walks as in (
21
), we shall distinguish between multigraphs
H
in which all edges are part of a cycle that can be contracted, and those for which this is not the case. To this end, we call the core
H^{\circ}
associated to a given
H

trivial
if it only contains a single vertex, and
non-trivial
otherwise.

It is easy to see that a given
H
has a trivial core if and only if all of its edges were removed after step 1 of the above algorithm, i.e., if and only if all its edges are part of some cycle in
L
. For this case, we define the
skeleton

\widetilde{H}
of
H
as the version of
H
were all edges in degenerate cycles on vertices
u,v
are replaced by exactly two edges between
u,v
. It is furthermore easy to see that
|\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]|\leqslant|\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(\widetilde{H})\right]|
since

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]|=\prod_{e\in L}|\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\right]|

due to independence of the removed cycles, and since the signed weight of any degenerate cycle is at most as large as the signed weight of a degenerate cycle with exactly two edges. This observation allows us to simplify some of the following calculations as accounting for the correct number of edges becomes easier if we know that each degenerate cycle contains exactly two edges.

Bounding
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
.

For every contracted edge
e\in E_{C}(H^{\circ})
we denote by
C_{e}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})
the chain in
H
that was contracted into
e
.
Similarly, for
e\in L
, we denote by
\textsc{Sw}(e)
the signed weight of the contracted cycle represented by
e
.
For every
e\in E_{C}
, we further denote by
\kappa_{e}
the average first mixed cumulant across all
\mathbf{z}_{i}
associated to a
\mathbf{z}
(cf.
Section

3.2
) that represents the chain
C_{e}
as defined in
Theorem

2.4
.
Using these notations, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right)\left(\prod_{\tilde{e}\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\prod_{e\in C_{\tilde{e}}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right)\left(\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\right)\right].

Now the plan is to use a high-probability event
\mathcal{G}

defined in terms of the positions of the vertices in
H^{\circ}
such that—conditional on
\mathcal{G}
—the contribution of the second factor above is small and can be bounded using
Theorem

2.6
.
The statement of
Theorem

2.6
directly suggests to define
\mathcal{G}
as the event that
\kappa_{e}
is small for all pairs of vertices in
H^{\circ}
.
In fact, the
\kappa_{e}
associated to our contracted chains are typically
9
9

9

Typically here is to be understood w.r.t. the randomness over the positions of the vertices in
U
.
and even with high probability of order
\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{d})
and thus much smaller than a worst-case placement of the vertices of
U
would imply. This suggests defining
\mathcal{G}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\bigcap_{e\in E_{C}}\left\{|\kappa_{e}|\leqslant\frac{\log^{2}(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}.

Using standard Bernstein bounds, we further conclude that
G
happens with high probability.

Lemma 8.5
.

We have
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\ell^{2}n^{-\Omega(\log(n))}.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.2
, we get that for a single
\kappa_{e}
,

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{k+1}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(|\kappa_{e}|\geqslant\frac{\log^{2}(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\leqslant n^{-\Omega(\log(n))}

Doing a union bound over all
\leqslant\ell^{2}
pairs of vertices concludes the proof.
∎

Conditioning on
\mathcal{G}
allows us to use the bounds from
Theorem

2.6
in essentially their strongest possible form.
However,
Theorem

2.6
only allows us to bound the contribution of contracted chains and cycles; to handle the case of general
p
, we must also control the influence of the non-contracted edges in
E_{U}
.
Our approach here is to note that—if
p
is small—most edges in
E_{U}
are not present, and every non-present edge only contributes a factor of magnitude
p
to the product in
\textsc{Sw}(H)
.
Hence
\textsc{Sw}(U)
(where we recall that
U
is the graph induced by all non-contracted edges of
H
, i.e., those in
E_{U}
) is typically of order
p^{t}
if
t
is the number of non-edges in
U
.

To make this idea formal, we shall refine the ‘good’ event
\mathcal{G}
further.
To this end, we fix a suitable spanning subgraph
F
of
U
. Precisely,
F
is just a spanning forest of
U
, plus an arbitrary additional edge from
E_{U}
closing a cycle whenever this is possible.

Denote the number of edges in
F
by
t
and define for every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,t\}
the event
\mathcal{E}_{i}
to be the event that the vertices of
H^{\circ}
are arranged such that exactly
i
edges in
E(F)
are present. We further denote by
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{|V(H^{\circ})|})
and
\overline{\mathbf{X}}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{|V(H)\setminus V(H^{\circ})|})
the positions of the vertices in
H^{\circ}
and outside of
H^{\circ}
, respectively. Moreover, let
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})=\textup{Unif}\left(\left[-1/2,1/2\right]\right)^{|V(H^{\circ})|}
and
\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})=\textup{Unif}\left(\left[-1/2,1/2\right]\right)^{|V(H^{\circ})\setminus V(H)|}
denote the (uniform) law of
\mathbf{X}
and
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
, respectively.

Lemma 8.6

(Bounding the Contribution of Non-Contracted Edges)
.

Fix a spanning subgraph
F
of
U
as described above and denote by
t
its number of edges. Further denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})
the law of
\mathbf{X}
conditional on
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\underset{(\mathbf{X},\overline{\mathbf{X}})\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})\otimes\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 2p^{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t}\ell^{j}\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\lvert\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\;\right|\;\mathbf{X}\right]\right\rvert\right]+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}).

Proof.

First of all, note that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\underset{(\mathbf{X},\overline{\mathbf{X}})\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})\otimes\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\hskip 17.07182pt\left\lvert\underset{(\mathbf{X},\overline{\mathbf{X}})\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})\otimes\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{G})\right]\right\rvert\hskip 19.91684pt+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{t}\left\lvert\underset{(\mathbf{X},\overline{\mathbf{X}})\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})\otimes\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})\right]\right\rvert+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}).

Now, since
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{E}_{j}
are defined only in terms of
\mathbf{X}
but not in terms of
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\lvert\underset{(\mathbf{X},\overline{\mathbf{X}})\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X})\otimes\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\left\lvert\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\hskip 5.69046pt\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\left\lvert\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\hskip 5.69046pt\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right)\left(\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\right)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\left\lvert\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\right|\hskip 1.42271pt\mathbf{X}\right]\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\lvert\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\right|\hskip 1.42271pt\mathbf{X}\right]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)p^{t-j}\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\lvert\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\right|\hskip 1.42271pt\mathbf{X}\right]\right\rvert\right]

because
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)
and since for any
\mathbf{X}
in
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}
, there are exactly
j
edges in our spanning subgraph
F
of
U
(whose edges exists as a function of
\mathbf{X}
), implying that
\left\lvert\prod_{e\in E_{U}}(\mathds{1}(e)-p)\right\rvert\leqslant p^{t-j}
.
Regarding the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{j}
, we note that since
F
is a forest plus a single edge closing a cycle, the edges outside the cycle appear independently of the edges within the cycle. Furthermore, for edges within the cycle, we can just apply
Theorem

2.6
combined with
Lemma

5.1
and basic inclusion-exclusion to recover the probability that any subset of edges is present or not present. Carrying out this calculation yields that the number of edges present in
F
– denoted by
X_{F}
– is still very close to being binomial in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{j}\right)\leqslant 2\binom{t}{j}p^{j}(1-p)^{t-j}\leqslant 2(pt)^{j}\leqslant 2(p\ell)^{j},

and the result follows.
∎

Now, we can use
Theorem

2.6
to bound the inner expectation above. In the following, we will denote the number of cycles contracted while constructing
H^{\circ}
by
s
, the number of contracted chains by
c
, and the number of edges in
H^{\circ}
by
r
. We further denote the number of degenerate cycles removed from
H
by
s_{d}
and the number of non-degenerate cycles by
s_{n}
, such that
s=s_{d}+s_{n}
. We recall that a cycle is degenerate if it consists of only two vertices, and that
H^{\circ}
is called trivial if it only consists of a single vertex. We proceed by bounding the signed weight of multigraphs
H
with trivial and non-trivial core separately.

Lemma 8.7

(Bounding the signed weight of
H
with trivial core)
.

Consider a Eulerian multigraph
H
such that the core
H^{\circ}
is trivial and its skeleton
\widetilde{H}
has
\ell
edges. Assume further that exactly
s
cycles where removed during construction of
H^{\circ}
(
|L|=s
) out of which
a
are degenerate.
Then, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\leqslant C^{s}p^{\ell-a}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2s}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that for a
H
with trivial core all edges are part of some removed cycle. Furthermore, all removed cycles are arranged in a tree-like fashion and therefore independent of each other. Denoting by
L_{d}
and
L_{n}
the set of removed degenerate and non-degenerate cycles, respectively, we immediately get from
Theorem

2.6
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(\widetilde{H})\right]\leqslant p^{a}\prod_{e\in L_{n}}Cp^{|C_{e}|}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{|C_{e}|-2}

where we accounted for the contribution of degenerate cycles using the factor of
p^{a}
since two edges between two vertices
u,v
in
\widetilde{H}
that form a degenerate cycle refer to the same underlying edge in
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
, implying that the associated signed weight is
p(1-p)^{2}+p^{2}(1-p)\leqslant p
. Summing together the respective exponents then finishes the proof.
∎

For non-trivial
H
, we prove a slightly different bound using the following two lemmas.

Lemma 8.8

(Bounding the contribution of contracted edges)
.

Consider a Eulerian multigraph
H
with
\ell
edges such that the core
H^{\circ}
contains
r
edges (
|E(H^{\circ})|=r
), there are
c
edges obtained from contracting exactly
c
chains (
|E_{C}|=c
), and such that
s
cycles where removed during construction of
H^{\circ}
(
|L|=s
) out of which
s_{d}
are degenerate.
Then, for any
\mathbf{X}
consistent with
\mathcal{G}
, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\right|\hskip 1.42271pt\mathbf{X}\right]\leqslant(C\log^{2}(n))^{c}p^{\ell-r+c-s}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}.

Proof.

Note that exactly
\ell-r+c
edges in
H
are part of a cycle or a chain that was contracted. Since we assumed that the vertices in
H^{\circ}
are positioned such that
\mathcal{G}
is met (i.e. since we assumed that
\mathbf{X}
is consistent with
\mathcal{G}
), we can apply
Theorem

2.6
with
|\kappa|\leqslant\log^{2}(n)/\sqrt{d}
. Using further that each chain and cycle represented by some
e\in E_{C}\cup L
is independent of all other chains and cycles by the way we constructed
H^{\circ}
, we can apply
Theorem

2.6
to each
C_{e}
separately and obtain

\displaystyle\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\hskip 2.84544pt\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\right|\hskip 1.42271pt\mathbf{X}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{e\in E_{C}}Cp^{|C_{e}|}\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{|C_{e}|-1}\times\prod_{e\in L}Cp^{|C_{e}|-1}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{|C_{e}|-2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\log^{2}(n))^{c}p^{\ell-r+c-s}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}.

Notice that we used
p^{|C_{e}|-1}
for
e\in L
above, instead of
p^{|C_{e}|}
as
Theorem

2.6
would allow for
k\geqslant 3
. The reason for this is that that we also have to account for cycles of length
k=2
, where a single vertex
v
with only one neighbor
u
and
a\geqslant 2
edges between
u,v
is removed as a (degenerate) cycle. In this case only a single factor of
p
is allowed instead of
p^{2}
, since the
a
edges between
u,v
refer to the same underlying edge in
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
, implying that the associated signed weight is
p(1-p)^{a}+p^{a}(1-p)\leqslant p(1-p)^{2}+p^{2}(1-p)\leqslant p
. Subtracting
1
in the exponent accounts for this in all cases.
∎

Using this in combination with
Lemma

8.6
, we get the following general bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]
for
H
with non-trivial core.

Lemma 8.9

(The signed weight of non-trivial Eulerian multigraphs)
.

Assume that
H
is a Eulerian multigraph with
\ell
edges and non-trivial core
H^{\circ}
. Assume further that
s
cycles were contracted when constructing
H^{\circ}
, and that
|E(H^{\circ})|=r
and
|V(H^{\circ})|=v
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\ell\log^{2}(n))^{\ell}p^{s+v}\left(\frac{p\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

Proof.

Using
Lemma

8.6
and
Lemma

8.8
yields that for any
H\in\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 2p^{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t}\ell^{j}\underset{\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{X}\mid\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\lvert\underset{\overline{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\prod_{e\in E_{C}\cup L}\textsc{Sw}(C_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\;\right|\;\mathbf{X}\right]\right\rvert\right]+\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\ell^{t}(C\log^{2}(n))^{\ell}p^{\ell-r+c-s+t}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}+\ell^{2}n^{-\Omega(\log(n))}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\ell^{t}(C\log^{2}(n))^{\ell}p^{c+s+t}\left(\frac{p\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s},

where
t
is the number of edges in a suitable spanning subgraph
F
of
U=H^{\circ}[E_{U}]
and
c=|E_{C}|
is the number of chains we contracted while constructing
H^{\circ}
. To finish the proof, we claim that
c+t\geqslant v
and we use the following case distinction to prove this. In case 1,
U
contains at least two components, so we know that
c+t\geqslant v
since all (say
C(U)
) components of
U
are joined together by
\geqslant C(U)
edges from
E_{C}
, otherwise
H^{\circ}
would be a tree and hence not Eulerian. In case 2,
U
only has one component. Here, we consider the following two sub-cases. Either
U
itself is a tree (possibly containing some multi-edges), in which case
t=v-1
. But we also know that
c\geqslant 1
as otherwise contracting cycles would have eliminated all edges and
H^{\circ}
would be an isolated vertex, contradicting the assumption that
H^{\circ}
is non-trivial. This implies
t+c\geqslant v
as desired. If
U
is not a tree, then
F
can be constructed as a spanning tree plus an additional edge, implying that
t\geqslant v
as well. Hence, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\ell^{t}(C\log^{2}(n))^{\ell}p^{s+v}\left(\frac{p\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s},

as desired.
∎

Putting everything together.

We prove the following bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]
.

Lemma 8.10

(Bounding the
m
-th power of the centered adjacency matrix)
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for all even
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geqslant 4
, and
d\ll np\log(n)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]\leqslant\begin{cases}(Cm)^{4m}\left(d+\log(n)^{2m+4}\right)\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}&\text{if }d\ll np\log^{2}(n)\\
(Cm)^{4m+2}\left(n+\log(n)^{2m}\right)\sqrt{np}^{m}&\text{if }d\gg np\log^{2}(n).\end{cases}

In particular, for every fixed
m
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]=\begin{cases}O\left(d\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)&\text{if }d\ll np\log^{2}(n)\\
O\left(n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)&\text{if }d\gg np\log^{2}(n).\end{cases}

Proof.

We denote by
\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
the set of all Eulerian multigraphs
H
with
V(H)\subseteq[n]
such that
|E(H)|=\ell
,
|V(H^{\circ})|=v
,
|L|=s
, and
|E(H^{\circ})|=r
.
Further, let
\mathcal{U}(\ell,r,s,v)\coloneqq\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)\,\big/\simeq
, the quotient set of
\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
with respect to graph isomorphism.
Note that, for any
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
with
H\simeq H^{\prime}
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H^{\prime})\right]
because, by symmetry,
\textsc{Sw}(H)
and
\textsc{Sw}(H^{\prime})
are identically distributed.
Hence, given an equivalence class
[H]\in\mathcal{U}(\ell,r,s,v)
, we allow ourselves to write
\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}([H])\right]
for the expected signed weight of any representative of the class
[H]
.

Our goal is to bound the right-hand side of (
21
) by summing over all possible choices of parameters
\ell,s,r,
and
v
, and all equivalence classes
[H]\in\mathcal{U}(\ell,r,s,v)
. To this end, we split the right-hand side of (
21
) into the contribution of those
H
with trivial, and non-trivial core, respectively. To this end, we further refine
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{U}
by defining
\mathcal{H}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)
as the set of all Eulerian multigraphs
H
with
\leqslant m
edges and trivial core whose
skeleton

\widetilde{H}
contains
\ell
edges and such that
|L|=s
, and
|L_{d}|=a
where
L_{d}
is the set of removed degenerate cycles. Moreover, we let
\mathcal{H}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)
be set of all Eulerian multigraphs
H
with non-trivial core such that
|E(H)|=\ell
,
|V(H^{\circ})|=v
,
|L|=s
, and
|E(H^{\circ})|=r
. We further define the quotient sets w.r.t. to multigraph isomorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)=\mathcal{H}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)\,\big/\simeq
, and
\mathcal{U}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)=\mathcal{H}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)\,\big/\simeq
. Note that any
H\in\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
is either in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{triv}}(\ell^{\prime},s)
or in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)
for some choice of parameters
\ell^{\prime},r,s,v,a\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell\}
. With this in mind, writing
\#[H]
for the number of walks
\boldsymbol{v}\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)
such that
H_{\boldsymbol{v}}\in[H]
, and
\#[\widetilde{H}]
for the number of walks
\boldsymbol{v}\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)
such that
\widetilde{H}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\in[H]
(where
\widetilde{H}_{\boldsymbol{v}}
is the
skeleton
of
H_{\boldsymbol{v}}
), we obtain from (
21
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\textsc{Ew}(n,m)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant S_{\text{triv}}+S_{\text{non-triv}}

where
S_{\text{triv}}
and
S_{\text{non-triv}}
account for all
H
with trivial and non-trivial core, respectively. Precisely, we express

\displaystyle S_{\text{triv}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}\sum_{a=0}^{s-1}\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{[H]\in\mathcal{U}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)}\#[\widetilde{H}]\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}([H])\right]\right\rvert+\sum_{[H]\in\mathcal{U}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor,\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor)}\#[\widetilde{H}]\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}([H])\right]\right\rvert\right)

where the first term in the parentheses accounts for all
H
with trivial core whose skeleton
\widetilde{H}
does not consist purely of degenerate cycles (which is why the second sum only goes until
s-1
). The second term accounts for those
H
whose skeleton
\widetilde{H}
consists only of degenerate cycles. Since it is easy to see that such
\widetilde{H}
can only exist if
\ell
is even and the number of cycles is
\ell/2
. Therefore, the above sums cover all cases for
H
with trivial core. On the other hand, for those
H
with non-trivial core, we use

\displaystyle S_{\text{non-triv}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sum_{v=1}^{\max\{r/2,1\}}\sum_{[H]\in\mathcal{U}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)}\#[H]\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}([H])\right]\right\rvert

where we used that the number of cycles
s
is at most
\ell/2
, and that the number of remaining edges
r
in the core
H^{\circ}
is at most
\ell-2s
since each cycle contains at least two edges. We further used that
H^{\circ}
contains at most
\max\{1,r/2\}
vertices since it has minimum degree
4
.

We proceed by bounding
S_{\text{triv}}
and
S_{\text{non-triv}}
separately. Starting with
S_{\text{triv}}
, we note that any
H\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)
has
1+\ell-s
vertices, and that there are
\leqslant\binom{m}{\ell}
ways of removing self-loops and excess edges in degenerate cycles when constructing
\widetilde{H}_{\boldsymbol{v}}
. Hence, we get that
\#[\widetilde{H}]\leqslant\binom{m}{\ell}n^{1+\ell-s}
. Moreover,
|\mathcal{U}_{\text{triv}}(\ell,s,a)|\leqslant m^{2m}
by a crude upper bound on the number of unlabeled multigraphs with
m
edges. Hence, applying
Lemma

8.7
, we get

\displaystyle S_{\text{triv}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}\sum_{a=0}^{s-1}n^{1+\ell-s}p^{\ell-a}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2s}+n^{1+\ell/2}p^{\ell/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}n\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}n^{\ell-s}p^{\ell-s+1}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2s}+\sqrt{np}^{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}\sqrt{np}^{2s}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2s}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}.

Similarly, applying
Lemma

8.9
and using that any
H\in\mathcal{H}(\ell,r,s,v)
has
\ell-r-s+v
vertices by
Lemma

8.4
, we get for
S_{\text{non-triv}}
that

\displaystyle S_{\text{non-triv}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sum_{v=1}^{\max\{r/2,1\}}\sum_{[H]\in\mathcal{U}_{\text{non-triv}}(\ell,r,s,v)}\#[H]\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}([H])\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant m^{4m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sum_{v=1}^{r/2}n^{\ell-r-s+v}(C\log^{2}(n))^{\ell}p^{s+v}\left(\frac{p\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sum_{v=1}^{r/2}(np)^{s+v}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sqrt{np}^{2s+r}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}.

Now, notice that both of the above upper bounds undergo a phase transition at
d=np\log(n)
: if
d\ll np\log^{2}(n)
, then
\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\gg\sqrt{np}
, so our sums are maximized when
s,r
are minimal. If on the other hand
d\gg np\log^{2}(n)
, then the opposite occurs and the sums are maximized when
s,r
are maximized. Distinguishing these two cases, we obtain our statement.

The case
\bm{d\ll np\log^{2}(n)}

Here, the sums are maximized when
s,r
are minimal, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}np\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}(\sqrt{np})^{2s+r}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(Cm)^{3m+1}\left(n^{2}p^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{np}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)+(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}(\sqrt{np})^{2s+4}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-4+2s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(Cm)^{3m+1}\left(d\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)+(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}n^{2}p^{2}\frac{d^{2}}{n^{4}p^{4}}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(Cm)^{4m}\left(d+\log(n)^{2m+4}\right)\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}=O\left(d\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right),

which confirms the intuition that there are
\approx d
eigenvalues each of order
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)
.

The case
\bm{d\gg np\log^{2}(n)}

In this case, the sums are maximized when
s,r
are maximal because
\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\ll\sqrt{np}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}\sqrt{np}^{2s}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-2s}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}(\sqrt{np})^{2s+r}\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sqrt{np}^{\ell}+(Cm)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}+(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}(\sqrt{np})^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{3m+2}n\sqrt{np}^{m}+(Cm^{4}\log^{2}(n))^{m}m^{2}(\sqrt{np})^{m}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm)^{4m+2}\left(n+\log(n)^{2m}\right)\sqrt{np}^{m}=O\left(n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right),

which confirms our intuition that now there are
\approx n
large eigenvalues, each of order
\sqrt{np}
like in a
G\left(n,p\right)
.
∎

Combining everything, we can prove
Lemma

8.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.1
.

By
Lemma

8.10
and (
1
) from the discussion of the trace method in
Section

2.6.3
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\geqslant a^{1/m}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}

with probability at most
1/a
. Choosing any
a=\omega(1)
and applying
Lemma

8.10
, we get that for any even
m
and assuming
d\ll np\log^{2}(n)
, with probability
1-o(1)
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\leqslant(Cm)^{4}\left(d^{1/m}+\log(n)^{3}\right)\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}+(Cm)^{4}n^{1/m}\sqrt{np}

where we used that
(a+b)^{1/m}\leqslant a^{1/m}+b^{1/m}
. Letting
m
tend to infinity, this is
n^{o(1)}\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}
as desired. In case
d\gg np\log^{2}(n)
, a similar calculation involving
Lemma

8.10
and letting
m
tend to infinity yields that
\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\leqslant n^{o(1)}\sqrt{np}
. Finally, to show that the number of large eigenvalues is bounded, we note that for every fixed, even
m

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}})^{m}=O\left(d\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right).

Assuming that
d\leqslant np^{1+\varepsilon}
, we can choose
m\geqslant\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+2
such that the above is
O\left(d\left(\frac{np\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\right)
. To further ensure that
m
is even, we require
m\geqslant\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+4
. Then, if there were more than
\widetilde{O}\left(da^{m}\right)
eigenvalues of magnitude
\geqslant\frac{np}{a\sqrt{d}}
, this would lead to a contradiction. ∎

8.2
A Different Threshold for
L_{\infty}
-norm

We complement the results from
Section

8.1
by studying the spectrum of
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
.
Here, it turns out that – perhaps surprisingly – the spectral threshold (i.e. the point at which the spectral gap is essentially as large as in a
G\left(n,p\right)
) is rather different and in particular reached much earlier than in the
L_{q}
model for fixed
1\leqslant q<\infty
.
Concretely, we show that the second largest eigenvalue of a typical
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
is of order
\tilde{\Theta}\left(\max\{\sqrt{np},np/d\}\right)
implying that the spectral threshold is reached at
d\approx\sqrt{np}
, instead of
d\approx np
as in the
L_{q}
model.
We can also characterize the spectrum of
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
in a somewhat different way: Instead of finding
\tilde{\Theta}(d)
eigenvectors with large eigenvalue if
d\ll np
, we can find
\tilde{\Theta}(d^{2})
such vectors, provided that
d\ll\sqrt{np}
(this is done in
Section

8.3.2
).
These results are combined the following theorem.
See
1.4

Proof.

The upper bound follows from
Lemma

8.13
and the lower bound from
Lemma

8.19
if
d\ll\sqrt{np}
. Otherwise, the lower bound follows from the generalized Alon–Boppana bound from
(
22
, Theorem 8)
using
r=1
.
∎

8.2.1
Bounding the Signed Weight of Cycles and Chains

Our proof has the same outline as the proof for the
L_{q}
model, so it essentially relies on the idea of bounding the signed weight of cycles and chains. The signed weight of a cycle has already been studied in previous work
[
2
]
, which we extend to also account for chains. To this end, recall that
\xi
is defined such that
(1-\xi)^{d}=p
, or alternatively such that
u\nsim v
whenever
|\mathbf{x}_{u}(i)-\mathbf{x}_{v}(i)|_{C}>\frac{1-\xi}{2}
. With this, we define the ‘good’ event
\mathcal{G}
in terms of the position of the two endpoints
u,v
of a fixed chain
H
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{G}_{i}\text{ occurs for}\geqslant d-\log^{2}(n)\text{ dimensions}\}\text{ where }\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq\left\{|\mathbf{x}_{u}(i)-\mathbf{x}_{v}(i)|_{C}\geqslant k\xi\right\}.

That is, under
\mathcal{G}
, there are at most
\log^{2}(n)
dimensions in which the endpoints of our chain
H
are within distance
\leqslant k\nu
. We call dimensions for which
\mathcal{G}_{i}
occurs
good
and all other dimensions
bad
. It can immediately be shown that it is very unlikely to have many bad dimensions.

Lemma 8.11
.

For all
k=o(\log^{2}(n))
it holds that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant n^{-\Omega(\log^{2}(n))}
.

Proof.

Applying a Taylor series to
\xi
, it is easy to note that
\xi=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d)
.
Therefore, the probability that a single, fixed dimension is bad is
O\left(k/d\right)
, and the number
X
of bad dimensions is the sum of independent Bernoulli random variables, each with success probability
O\left(k/d\right)
. Combining Remark 2.5 and Theorem 2.8 in
[
21
]
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}\left(X\geqslant t\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-t\right)\text{ for all }t\geqslant 7\mathbb{E}\left[X\right].

Noting that
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=O\left(k\right)
and
k=o(\log^{2}(n))
, the result follows after setting
t=\log^{2}(n)
.
∎

While the authors of
[
2
]
also give bounds on the signed weight of general graphs, these are not strong enough for our purposes since they do not yield tight bounds in the special case of chains. We prove the following while partly (i.e. in the case of cycles) relying on the bounds from
[
2
]
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Consider
G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
with
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
. Then, for a cycle
C_{k}
of length
k\geqslant 3
or a chain
H
with
k\geqslant 2
edges with endpoints
v_{1},v_{k+1}
and associated positions
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{k+1}
such that
\mathcal{G}
is met, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(C_{k})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\log(n)p^{k}\left(\frac{2\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-2}

(22)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\mid\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{k+1}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\log^{2}(n)p^{k}\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}.

(23)

Proof.

Regarding the cycle, we rely on
(
2
, Proposition 3.5)
, which immediately yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(C_{k})\right]\leqslant p^{k}\left(1+(1+o(1))d\xi^{k-1}\right)\leqslant p^{k}\left(1+3\log(n)\left(\frac{2\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-2}\right)

since applying a Taylor series to
\xi=1-p^{1/d}
yields that
