\displaystyle\xi=1-p^{1/d}=1-\left(1-\frac{\log(1/p)}{d}\pm O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(n)}{d^{2}}\right)\right)=\frac{\log(1/p)}{d}\pm O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(n)}{d^{2}}\right)\leqslant\frac{2\log(1/p)}{d}.

(24)

For the chain
H
, we use a ‘cluster expansion’ based approach based on the same idea as in
[
2
]
. This is the same approach also used to derive (
22
), however, in case of a chain
H
, we have to use a slight modification to account for the fact that the endpoints of
H
are in
\mathcal{G}
. To carry this out, we start by noting that our chain exists if and only if it exists in all
d
dimensions. Formally, this allows us to rewrite

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]=\left(\underset{G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]\right)^{d}.

We bound
\underset{G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]
by expressing it as an alternating sum over all subsets
A\subseteq H
where each term is (up to its sign) equal to the probability of the event that no edge in
A
is present. Accordingly, we define

\displaystyle\chi(A)\coloneqq\underset{G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcap_{e\in E(A)}(\mathds{1}(e)=0)\right)

and note that by elementary inclusion-exclusion,

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]=\sum_{A\subseteq H}(-1)^{|E(A)|}\chi(A).

Now to quantify how close this expression is to
p^{k}
, we measure ‘perturbations’ from a
G\left(n,p\right)
by defining

\displaystyle\psi(A)=\chi(A)-\xi^{|E(A)|}.

As the probability that no edge in
A
is present in a
G\left(n,p\right)
of the same density (i.e. density
p^{1/d}
) is exactly
\xi^{|E(A)|}
, the quantity
\psi(A)
measures how much the probabilities of the event in question differ between
G\left(n,p\right)
and
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)
. We now handle good and bad dimensions separately.

Good dimensions.

Recall that dimension
i
is good if the endpoints
u,v
of our chain
H
are such that
|\mathbf{x}_{u}(i)-\mathbf{x}_{v}(i)|_{C}\geqslant k\xi
.
Hence, we consider the
1
-dimensional model
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)
conditioned on the event that the dimension is good with respect to
H
.
We denote the associated random graph model by
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\big|\mathcal{G}_{1}
, and observe that, under its law, it holds that

\displaystyle\chi(A)=\begin{cases}\xi^{|E(A)|}&\text{if }E(A)\neq E(H)\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\text{ and thus }\psi(A)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }E(A)\neq E(H)\\
-\xi^{|E(A)|}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

if
d
is sufficiently large.
The reason for this is that a large dimension implies that
\xi=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d)
is very small and sufficiently distant endpoints (specifically endpoints at distance
\geqslant k\xi
) make it impossible to arrange all vertices such that all edges in
H
are excluded. A similar argument was also used in
[
2
]
, we specifically refer the interested reader to
(
2
, Claim 3.1, Item 4)
for further details.
With this in mind, we obtain

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\mathcal{G}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(-1)^{\ell}\sum_{A\subseteq H:|A|=\ell}\left(\xi^{\ell}+\psi(A)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}\xi^{-\ell}\right)-(-1)^{k}\xi^{k}=\left(1-\xi\right)^{k}-(-1)^{k}\xi^{k}.

Noting that
1-\xi=p^{1/d}
implies that

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\mathcal{G}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]\leqslant p^{1/d}\pm\xi^{k}=p^{1/d}\left(1\pm p^{-1/d}\xi^{k}\right).

Finally, applying the Taylor series of
\xi=1-p^{1/d}
from (
24
) and using further that
p^{-1/d}=1+o(1)
since
p\geqslant n^{-O\left(1\right)}
and
d=\omega(\log(n))
, we get that

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\mathcal{G}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]\leqslant p^{k/d}\left(1+\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k}\right).

Bad dimensions.

For all bad dimensions, we use the same strategy but rely on a slightly weaker bound on
\chi(H)
obtained by fixing an arbitrary
A\subset H
with
|E(A)|=k-1
and noting that

\displaystyle\chi(H)

\displaystyle=\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcap_{e\in E(H)}(\mathds{1}(e)=0)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcap_{e\in E(A)}(\mathds{1}(e)=0)\right)=\xi^{k-1}

since edges in all proper subsets of
H
are still independent. Therefore, we still have
\psi(A)=0
if
A\subsetneq H
and
\psi(A)=\xi^{k-1}-\xi^{k}
. Carrying out the same calculation as before yields that

\displaystyle\underset{G\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}(-1)^{k}\xi^{k}\right)+(-1)^{k}\left(\xi^{k-1}-\xi^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\xi\right)^{k}+(-1)^{k}\left(\xi^{k-1}-\xi^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{1/d}+\xi^{k-1}\leqslant p^{k/d}\left(1+\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}\right).

Putting everything together.

In total, we obtain

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)\right|\mathcal{G}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\mathcal{G}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]\right)^{d-\log^{2}(n)}\left(\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\left.\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,1,p^{1/d}\right)\right|\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in H}\mathds{1}(e)\right]\right)^{\log^{2}(n)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(p^{k/d}\left(1+\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k}\right)\right)^{d-\log^{2}(n)}\left(p^{k/d}\left(1+\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}\right)\right)^{\log^{2}(n)}

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{k}\left(1+d\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{d}\binom{d}{\ell}\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell k}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\left(1+\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\log^{2}(n)}\binom{\log^{2}(n)}{\ell}\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell(k-1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{k}\left(1+2d\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k}\right)\left(1+2\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant p^{k}\left(1+3\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{3\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{k-1}\right).

where the penultimate step used that the sums decay exponentially due to
d=n^{\Omega(1)}
and
p\geqslant 1/n
.
∎

8.2.2
Using The Trace Method (Again)

Here, we prove the following upper bound on the second largest eigenvalue of
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Consider a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
. With probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}\leqslant\log^{11}(n)\max\left\{\sqrt{np},\frac{np}{d}\right\}

where
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})
are the eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
of a
\mathbf{G}
. Moreover, if
d\ll p^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{np}
, the number of Eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A})
with
|\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\geqslant\frac{np}{ad}
is at most
\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}a^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+4}\right)
.

The proof is essentially the same as described in more detail in
Section

8.1
: Expanding the expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]
, we get a sum over the signed weight of Eulerian multigraphs
H
with
\ell\leqslant m
edges.
For every such
H
, we construct a core
H^{\circ}
with minimum degree
4
by repeatedly contracting all cycles and chains. Then, in order to bound the signed weight of
H
, we treat the non-contracted edges of
H^{\circ}
and the contracted cycles and chains from
H
separately. In this regard, it is not hard to see that
Lemma

8.6
carries over verbatim to the
L_{\infty}
model. Then, it is not hard to obtain the following analogs of
Lemma

8.7
and
Lemma

8.9
, essentially by replacing
Theorem

2.6
by
Lemma

8.12
.

Lemma 8.14

(Bounding the signed weight of
H
with trivial core)
.

Consider a Eulerian multigraph
H
such that the core
H^{\circ}
is trivial and its skeleton
\widetilde{H}
has
\ell
edges. Assume further that exactly
s
cycles where removed during construction of
H^{\circ}
(
|L|=s
) out of which
a
are degenerate.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\leqslant(3\log(n))^{s}p^{\ell-a}\left(\frac{2\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-2s}.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
Lemma

8.7
with
Lemma

8.12
instead of
Theorem

2.6
.
∎

Lemma 8.15

(The signed weight of non-trivial Eulerian multigraphs)
.

Assume that
H
is a Eulerian multigraph with
\ell
edges and non-trivial core
H^{\circ}
. Assume further that
s
cycles were contracted when constructing
H^{\circ}
, and that
|E(H^{\circ})|=r
and
|V(H^{\circ})|=v
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\textsc{Sw}(H)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\ell\log^{3}(n))^{\ell}p^{s+v}\left(\frac{3p\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
Lemma

8.9
with
Lemma

8.12
instead of
Theorem

2.6
.
∎

With this, we are ready to trace the trace once again.

Lemma 8.16

(Bounding the
m
-th power of the Centered Adjacency Matrix (Again))
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for all even
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]\leqslant\begin{cases}(C\log(n)m)^{4m+4}\left(d^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)&\text{if }d\ll\sqrt{np}\log(1/p)\\
(C\log(n)m)^{4m+2}\sqrt{np}^{m}&\text{if }d\gg\sqrt{np}\log(1/p).\end{cases}

Proof.

We define
S_{\text{triv}}
and
S_{\text{non-triv}}
as in the proof of
Lemma

8.10
. Applying
Lemma

8.14
and
Lemma

8.15
, then yields

\displaystyle S_{\text{triv}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell/2}\sqrt{np}^{2s}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-2s}+(C\log(n)m)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}

and

\displaystyle S_{\text{non-triv}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(Cm^{4}\log^{3}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}\sqrt{np}^{2s+r}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}.

Again, both of these bounds show a phase transition, this time at
d\approx\sqrt{np}\log(1/p)
.

The Case
d\ll\sqrt{np}\log(1/p)

Here, the sums are maximized when
s,r
are minimal, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{3m}np\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}np\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-2}+(C\log(n)m)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+(Cm^{4}\log^{3}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell/2}\sum_{r=4}^{\ell-2s}(\sqrt{np})^{2s+r}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell-r-2s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{3m+1}\left(n^{2}p^{2}\left(\frac{d}{np}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+(Cm^{4}\log^{4}(n))^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{m}n^{2}p^{2}\frac{d^{4}}{n^{4}p^{4}}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{4m}(d^{2}+\log^{4}(n))\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}+(C\log(n)m)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{4m+4}\left(d^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{\ell}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right).

The Case
d\gg\sqrt{np}\log(1/p)

In this case, sums are maximized when
s,r
are large, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{3m}mnp\sqrt{np}^{\ell}+(C\log(n)m)^{3m+1}n\sqrt{np}^{m}+(Cm^{4}\log^{3}(n))^{m}m^{2}(\sqrt{np})^{m}

\displaystyle\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{4m+2}\sqrt{np}^{m}=O\left(n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right),

as desired.
∎

Combining everything, we prove
Lemma

8.13
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.13
.

By the discussion about the trace method ((
1
) from
Section

2.6.3
), we have that

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\geqslant a^{1/m}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]^{1/m}

with probability at most
1/a
. Choosing any
a=\omega(1)
and applying
Lemma

8.10
, we get that for any even
m
and assuming
d\ll np\log(n)
, with probability
1-o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\leqslant(C\log(n)m)^{5}\left(d^{2/m}\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}+n^{1/m}\sqrt{np}\right)

where
C>0
is a constant. Choosing
m=\Omega(\log(n))
, this is
O\left(\log^{11}(n)np/d\right)
as desired. In case
d\gg\sqrt{np}\log(1/p)
,
Lemma

8.16
yields that
\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right|\leqslant\widetilde{O}\left(\log^{10}(n)\sqrt{np}\right)
. Finally, to show that the number of large eigenvalues is bounded, we note that if
d\leqslant p^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{np}
, then for every fixed even
m
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{tr}\left(\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{m}\right)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}})^{m}=\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}+n\sqrt{np}^{m}\right).

Assuming that
d\leqslant p^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{np}
, we can choose
m\geqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+2
such that the above is
\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}\left(\frac{np\log(1/p)}{d}\right)^{m}\right)
. To further ensure that
m
is even, we require
m\geqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+4
. Then, if there were more than
\widetilde{O}\left(d^{2}a^{m}\right)
eigenvalues of magnitude
\geqslant\frac{np}{ad}
, this would lead to a contradiction. ∎

8.3
Lower Bound: Finding Explicit Eigenvectors

In this section, we show that the upper bounds from
Lemma

8.1
and
Lemma

8.13
are tight up to lower order factors.

8.3.1
L_{q}
-Norms for
q<\infty

We complement the results from the previous subsection by showing that we can in fact find
d
approximate eigenvectors with eigenvalue
\Theta\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)
provided that
d\ll np
. Precisely, what we mean by ‘approximate eigenvectors’ is that we can find
d
unit vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}
such that

\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\mathbf{y}_{i}=\Theta\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

and the
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}
have pairwise inner products close to
0
, in the order of
1/\sqrt{n}
. It is easy to see that one can turn such a collection into
\Theta(d)
‘real’ eigenvectors, by applying Gram-Schmidt.

Lemma 8.17
.

Consider a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and assume that
d\ll np
. With probability
1-o(1)
, there are unit vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\mathbf{y}_{i}=\Theta\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\text{ and }\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{y}_{j}=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\text{ for all }i\neq j.

In particular this implies that

\displaystyle\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

The idea behind our construction is to define one vector
\mathbf{y}_{i}
for every dimension
i\in[d]
. Now, sample the position of all vertices in dimension
i
and call the result
\mathbf{x}_{(i)}
, i.e., the
n
-dimensional vector containing the position of all vertices in dimension
i
. Now, fix a sufficiently small constant
c>0
and define the set
A
as the set of all vertices which have distance at most
c
from the origin in dimension
i
, and similarly the set
B
as the set of all vertices with distance at least
\frac{1}{2}-c
from the origin. More formally, we set
A=\{v\mid|\mathbf{x}_{(i)}(v)|_{C}\leqslant c\}
and
B=\{v\mid|\mathbf{x}_{(i)}(v)|_{C}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}-c\}
. With this, we define the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}\in\{\pm 1,0\}
by setting

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}(v)=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }v\in A\\
-1&\text{if }v\in B\\
\hskip 6.544pt0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Finally,
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is defined as
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
normalized to unit length (i.e.,
\mathbf{y}_{i}\eqqcolon\mathbf{y}_{i}/\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}\|
). This construction is depicted in
Figure

1
, left.
The reason why this procedure yields an approximate eigenvector w.h.p. is that the quadratic form
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
is equal to the difference of the number of edges with both endpoints in either
A
or
B
(called
intra-cluster edges
), and the number of edges with one endpoint in
A
and the other in
B
(called
inter-cluster edges
).
Because intra-cluster edges are slightly likelier to be present than inter-cluster edges (w.r.t. the randomness incurred by all dimensions except
i
), this difference has expected size
\approx\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}
.
Since
d\ll np
, this is much larger than the magnitude of the random fluctuations in the number of inter- and intra-cluster edges (which is of order
\sqrt{np}
), so we can prove that
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{i}=\Omega(np/\sqrt{d})
holds w.h.p., as desired.
It is furthermore easy to see that the
\mathbf{y}_{i}
constructed this way are almost orthogonal.

Figure 1:
Illustration for the construction of explicit eigenvectors for a
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
and
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
. Left: The case of
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)
. It is indicated that intra-cluster edges (solid) are likelier than inter-cluster edges (dashed), making the vector constructed this way an approximate eigenvector. Right: The changes required for
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
. The sets
A,B
become much smaller and only take up a ‘volume’ of
\xi=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d
. Now we can fit
\tilde{\Omega}(d)
vectors in a single dimension. Furthermore, inter-cluster do not exist.

The crucial fact upon which the above discussion relies is that our intra-cluster edges are likelier to be present than inter-cluster edges.
This difference is of order
p/\sqrt{d}
.
We capture this formally in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.18
.

For all
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
, we have that

\displaystyle\forall\{u,v\}\in\binom{A}{2}\cup\binom{B}{2}:\mathbb{P}\left(u\sim v\mid\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant p+\frac{Cp}{\sqrt{d}}\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\forall\{u,v\}\in A\times B:\mathbb{P}\left(u\sim v\mid\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant p-\frac{Cp}{\sqrt{d}}.

Proof.

Denote by
r=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\Delta_{i}(\{u,v\})-\mu}{\sigma\sqrt{d}}
the rescaled
L_{q}
-distance (raised to the power of
q
) between two vertices
u
and
v
.
Define further
\bar{r}
by leaving out the first dimension in the previous sum.
We essentially use that, if
u,v
are either both in
A
or both in
B
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(u\sim v\mid\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(r\leqslant\hat{\tau}\mid\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{r}+\frac{c-\mu}{\sigma\sqrt{d}}\leqslant\hat{\tau}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}-\frac{c-\mu}{\sigma\sqrt{d}}\right)

because
u,v
both being in either
A
or
B
implies that
\Delta_{1}(\{u,v\})\leqslant c
.
We choose
c
sufficiently small (i.e. smaller than
\mu
) such that
\beta=-\frac{c-\mu}{\sigma\sqrt{d}}
is positive and focus on bounding
\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)
from below.
We apply an Edgeworth expansion to
\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}
using
Theorem

3.12
, where we added the factor of
\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}
to ensure that the variance remains
1
.
This yields that, for all
s\geqslant 2
,

\displaystyle f_{\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi(x)+\phi(x)\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{p_{j}(x)}{(d-1)^{j/2}}\right)\pm o\left(\frac{1}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\right)

(25)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi(x)+\phi(x)\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{p_{j}(x)}{d^{j/2}}\left(\frac{d}{d-1}\right)^{j/2}\right)\pm o\left(\frac{1}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\right)

(26)

Using a Taylor expansion, we get that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{d-1}\right)^{j/2}=\left(1+\frac{1}{d-1}\right)^{j/2}=1+\frac{j}{2(d-1)}\pm O\left(\frac{1}{d^{2}}\right).

Hence, for every
s\geqslant 2
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle f_{\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}}(x)

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(\phi(x)+\phi(x)\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{p_{j}(x)}{d^{j/2}}\right)-\phi(x)\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{C|x|^{3j}}{d^{1+j/2}}-o\left(\frac{1}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant f_{r}(x)-\phi(x)\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{C|x|^{3j}}{d^{1+j/2}}-o\left(\frac{1}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\right),

(27)

where we noted that the expression in the parentheses is just the
s
-th order Edgeworth expansion of
r
.
Now, we use that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}\leqslant-\log(n)\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(-\log(n)\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right).

From the tail bound in
Lemma

3.7
, we get that the first term is
n^{-\omega(1)}
.
Recalling that
|\hat{\tau}|\leqslant\log(n)
, defining
\alpha=-\log(n)
and integrating (
27
), we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(-\log(n)\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-1}}\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\alpha\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}\frac{C\log(n)^{3j}}{d^{1+j/2}}\int_{\alpha}^{\hat{\tau}+\beta}\phi(x)\text{d}x-o\left(\frac{\log(n)}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\alpha\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{s-2}O\left(\left(\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{j}\right)\Phi(\hat{\tau}+\beta)-\frac{p}{d}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\alpha\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)-(1+o(1))\frac{\Phi(\hat{\tau}+\beta)}{d}-\frac{p}{d}

if we choose
s
to be a sufficiently large constant such that
\frac{\log(n)}{d^{(s-2)/2}}\leqslant\frac{p}{d}
, which is possible since
d\geqslant n^{\gamma}
. We further note that

\displaystyle\Phi(\hat{\tau}+\beta)

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi(\hat{\tau})+\beta\sup_{x\in[\hat{\tau},\hat{\tau}+\beta]}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=p+\beta\sup_{x\in[\hat{\tau},\hat{\tau}+\beta]}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=p+\beta\phi(\hat{\tau})\sup_{y\in[0,\beta]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(2y\hat{\tau}+y^{2}\right)\right)=p+(1-o(1))\beta\phi(\hat{\tau})

as
\left\lvert\hat{\tau}\right\rvert\leqslant\log(n)
and
\beta=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=n^{-\Omega(1)}
.
Further, we have that
\phi(\hat{\tau})\leqslant Cp
by
Corollary

3.17
.
Combined this yields that
\Phi(\hat{\tau}+\beta)\leqslant p(1+O(1/\sqrt{d}))
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{r}\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\alpha\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)-O\left(\frac{p}{d}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{P}\left(r\leqslant\hat{\tau}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(r\leqslant\alpha\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\tau}\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)-O\left(\frac{p}{d}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant p-n^{-\omega(1)}-O\left(\frac{p}{d}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\tau}\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)

by the tail bound from
Lemma

3.7
.
Now, applying another Edgeworth expansion to
f_{r}
using
Theorem

3.12
yields that
\sup_{x\in[\hat{\tau},\hat{\tau}+\beta]}\left|\frac{f_{r}(x)}{\phi(x)}\right|=1\pm o(1)
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\tau}\leqslant r\leqslant\hat{\tau}+\beta\right)\geqslant(1-o(1))\beta\sup_{x\in[\hat{\tau},\hat{\tau}+\beta]}\phi(x)\geqslant(1-o(1))\beta\phi(\hat{\tau})=\Omega\left(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

as noted previously. This shows the first part of our claim, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(u\sim v\mid\mathbf{x}_{(1)})\geqslant p+\frac{Cp}{\sqrt{d}}
for
u,v
being either both in
A
or both in
B
. The second part follows by an analogous calculation.
∎

In total, this allows us to prove
Lemma

8.17
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.17
.

For a set
M\subseteq V
, we denote by
\deg(u\rightarrow M)
the number of neighbors of
u
in the set
M
.
It is not hard to notice that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}}\left(\sum_{u\in A}\left(\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\deg(u\rightarrow B)\right)+\sum_{u\in B}\left(\deg(u\rightarrow B)-\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right)\right).

In other words, evaluating the quadratic form
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
is the difference of the number of intra-cluster edges and inter-cluster edges.
To estimate the sum, we recall that the sets
A,B
are defined as a function of the position of all vertices in our fixed, first dimension, denoted by
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
.
We call
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}

good
if

\displaystyle\left\lvert|A|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 10\sqrt{n\log(n)}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\left\lvert|B|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|B|\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 10\sqrt{n\log(n)}.

Applying the Bernstein bound (
Theorem

3.13
), we see that
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good with probability
1-1/n^{2}
, as

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left||A|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]\right|\geqslant 10\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\leqslant 2\exp\left(\frac{-100n\log(n)}{2n+10\sqrt{n\log(n)}}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Now, we can apply
Lemma

8.18
to estimate
\deg(u\rightarrow A)
and the related terms. To this end, we first note that there is a constant
0<\alpha<1
such that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}\left([-1/2,1/2]^{n}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]=\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}\left([-1/2,1/2]^{n}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|B|\right]=\alpha n.

Further, assuming that
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good, we get by
Lemma

8.18
that, for any
u\in A
,

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]\geqslant p(\alpha n-10\sqrt{n\log(n)})+\Omega\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\geqslant\alpha pn+\Omega\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

since
d\ll(np)^{1-\varepsilon}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that the random variable
\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\mathbb{E}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]
(where the randomness is over the position of all vertices in dimensions
\{2,\ldots,d\}
while the positions in dimension
1
are fixed) can be represented as a sum of independent zero mean random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{|A|}
bounded in absolute value by
1
. Furthermore, each
X_{i}
can be represented as
X_{i}=Y_{i}-p_{i}
where
Y_{i}
is a Bernoulli RV with expectation
p_{i}
, while
p_{i}=(1+o(1))p
by
Lemma

8.18
. Hence,
X_{i}
takes a value of
1-p_{i}
w.p.
p_{i}
and a value of
-p_{i}
w.p.
1-p_{i}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i}^{2}\right]\leqslant p_{i}(1-p_{i})^{2}+(1-p_{i})p_{i}^{2}\leqslant 2p_{i}\leqslant 3p.

Thus, by a Bernstein bound (
Theorem

3.13
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]\right|\geqslant 10\sqrt{np\log(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{-100np\log(n)}{6p|A|+10\sqrt{np\log(n)}}\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{-100np\log(n)}{6np+10\sqrt{np\log(n)}}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

This certifies that there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\deg(u\rightarrow A)

\displaystyle\geqslant\alpha np+\Omega\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)-10\sqrt{np\log(n)}\geqslant\alpha np+\frac{Cnp}{\sqrt{d}}

holds with probability
1-1/n^{2}
over the randomness in
\mathrm{RGG}_{q}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
whenever we have a good
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
. Note that we also applied the fact that
d\ll(np)^{1-\varepsilon}
above. An entirely analogous calculation shows that
\deg(u\rightarrow B)\leqslant\alpha np-\frac{Cnp}{\sqrt{d}}
holds as well. Noting that
\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}\leqslant n
and that
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good w.p.
1-1/n^{2}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{1}\geqslant\frac{(|A|+|B|)2Cnp}{n\sqrt{d}}=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

holds with probability
1-2/n^{2}
. Since this is true for each
\mathbf{y}_{i}
, we get from a union bound that it also holds for all
\mathbf{y}_{i}
w.h.p.

We finish the proof by arguing that the constructed random vectors are w.h.p. pairwise almost orthogonal and that we can lift our lower bound on their quadratic forms from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
For the former, note that, for each
j
,
\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}\|^{2}
is a Binomial random variable with success probability bounded away from
0
and
n
trails, and therefore
\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}\|^{2}=\Omega(n)
with probability
1-O\left(n^{-3}\right)
.
Moreover, for every
i\neq j
,
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
is a sum of
n
independent random variables in
\{-1,0,1\}
, each with mean
0
.
Hence, by McDiarmid’s inequality, we get that
|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}|=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
with probability
1-O\left(n^{-3}\right)
.
Combining both yields that
|\mathbf{y}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{y}_{i}|=\widetilde{O}\left(1/\sqrt{n}\right)
for each
i\neq j
with probability
1-O\left(n^{-3}\right)
, and applying a union bound proves that w.h.p. all pairs of vectors are almost orthogonal.
To lift our bound on the quadratic form from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we further observe that, by McDiarmid’s inequality,
|\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}|=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
with probability
1-O\left(n^{-2}\right)
.
Hence, using again that
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}\|^{2}=\Omega(n)
with probability
1-O\left(n^{-3}\right)
, we can apply the union bound to obtain that w.h.p.

|\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}})\mathbf{y}_{i}|=p|\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{y}_{i}|^{2}=\widetilde{O}\left(p\right)\leqslant o\left(\frac{np}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

for all
i
.
∎

8.3.2
L_{\infty}
-Norm

For
L_{\infty}
-norm, we prove the following.

Lemma 8.19
.

Consider a
\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
and assume that
d\ll\sqrt{np}
. With probability
1-o(1)
, there are unit vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{r}
with
r=\Theta(d^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{A}-p\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\mathbf{y}_{1}=\Theta\left(\frac{np}{d}\right)\text{ and }\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{y}_{j}=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\text{ for all }i\neq j.

In particular this implies that

\displaystyle\max\{|\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{A})|,|\lambda_{n}(\mathbf{A})|\}=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{d}\right).

We construct the vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{r}
in a similar way as before. The difference will be that we do not get one vector per dimension, but
\tilde{\Theta}(d)
many. Again, we construct our vectors by sampling the position of all vertices in a fixed dimension, which we denote by
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
.
Now, we shall define a region within our fixed dimension that consists of two opposing circular arcs, each of length
\xi/2=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d)
as depicted in
Figure

1
, right.
Similarly to the notation used previously, we call the set of vertices falling into one of the arcs
A
, and the set of vertices falling into the other
B
.
Then, we define a vector
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
just like before, by setting

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(v)=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }v\in A\\
-1&\text{if }v\in B\\
\hskip 6.544pt0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Finally, our approximate eigenvector
\mathbf{y}
will be the normalized version of the above, i.e.,
\mathbf{y}=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}/\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\|
.
It is easy to see that—since the length of our circular arcs is only
\xi/2=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d)
—we can define
\Theta(d)
orthogonal such
\mathbf{y}

for each
dimension, such that in the end, we end up with
\Theta(d^{2})
vectors in total.
To prove that the vectors constructed this way are indeed approximate eigenvectors, we again show that inter-cluster edges are less likely than intra-cluster edges. In case of inter-cluster edges, this is in fact trivial, since it is easy to see that for
u\in A
and
v\in B
, the
L_{\infty}
-distance between
u,v
is above the threshold, no matter what happens in the remaining
d-1
dimensions. Accordingly, by construction of
A
and
B
, there are no inter-cluster edges. However, for intra-cluster edges, we prove the following simple lemma.

Lemma 8.20
.

For all
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
, we have that

\displaystyle\forall\{u,v\}\in\binom{A}{2}\cup\binom{B}{2}:\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(u\sim v\right)=p^{\frac{d-1}{d}}=p\left(1+\Omega\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)\right).

where
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
denotes the law of
\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)
conditional on fixing the positions of all vertices in dimension
1
according to
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
.

Proof.

Conditional on
u,v\in A
or
u,v\in B
for a fixed dimension, the probability that
u
and
v
are below distance
1-\xi
in each of the remaining
d-1
dimensions is exactly
(1-\xi)^{d-1}=p^{\frac{d-1}{d}}
. Applying a Taylor series then yields the desired result.
∎

With this, we can prove
Lemma

8.19
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.19
.

As in the proof of
Lemma

8.17
, for any set
M\subseteq V
, we denote by
\deg(u\rightarrow M)
the number of neighbors of
u
in
M
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}}\left(\sum_{u\in A}\left(\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\deg(u\rightarrow B)\right)+\sum_{u\in B}\left(\deg(u\rightarrow B)-\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}}\left(\sum_{u\in A}\deg(u\rightarrow A)+\sum_{u\in B}\deg(u\rightarrow B)\right)

because no inter-cluster edges exist. To estimate the sum, we recall that the sets
A,B
are defined as a function of the position of all vertices in our fixed, first dimension, denoted by
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
. We call
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}

good
if

\displaystyle\left\lvert|A|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\left\lvert|B|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|B|\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}.

Note that
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good with probability
1-1/n^{2}
as, by the Bernstein bound (
Theorem

3.13
),

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left||A|-\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}([-1/2,1/2]^{n})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]\right|\geqslant 10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt\leqslant 2\exp\left(\frac{-\frac{100n\log^{2}(n)}{d}}{n\xi+10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{n^{2}}

since
\xi=\Theta(\log(1/p)/d)
. Moreover, it is easy to note that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}\left([-1/2,1/2]^{n}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|A|\right]=\underset{\mathbf{x}_{(1)}\sim\text{Unif}\left([-1/2,1/2]^{n}\right)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|B|\right]=\frac{1}{2}\xi n.

Now, applying
Lemma

8.20
, we get that for every good
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
and any
u\in A
,

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant p|A|\geqslant p\left(\frac{1}{2}\xi n-10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\xi np-10p\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{d}\right)

since
d\ll\sqrt{np}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that the random variable
\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\mathbb{E}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]
(where the randomness is over the position of all vertices in dimensions
\{2,\ldots,d\}
while the positions in dimension
1
are fixed) can be represented as a sum of independent zero mean random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{|A|}
bounded in absolute value by
1
. Furthermore, each
X_{i}
can be represented as
X_{i}=Y_{i}-p_{i}
where
Y_{i}
is a Bernoulli RV with expectation
p_{i}
, while
p_{i}=(1+o(1))p
by
Lemma

8.20
. Hence,
X_{i}
takes a value of
1-p_{i}
w.p.
p_{i}
and a value of
-p_{i}
w.p.
1-p_{i}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i}^{2}\right]\leqslant p_{i}(1-p_{i})^{2}+(1-p_{i})p_{i}^{2}\leqslant 2p_{i}\leqslant 3p.

Thus, by a Bernstein bound (
Theorem

3.13
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|\deg(u\rightarrow A)-\underset{\mathbf{G}\sim\mathrm{RGG}_{\infty}\left(n,d,p\right)|\mathbf{x}_{(1)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\deg(u\rightarrow A)\right]\right|\geqslant 10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{np}{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{-100\frac{np\log^{2}(n)}{d}}{6p|A|+10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{np}{d}}}\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{-100\frac{np\log^{2}(n)}{d}}{6\xi np+10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{np}{d}}}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{n^{2}}

Where we used that
|A|\leqslant\xi np
because
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good. This certifies that

\displaystyle\deg(u\rightarrow A)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\xi np-10\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{np}{d}}-10p\log(n)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{d}\right)

holds with probability
1-1/n^{2}
whenever we have a good
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
. Note that we also applied the fact that
d\ll\sqrt{np}
above. An entirely analogous calculation shows that
\deg(u\rightarrow B)=\Omega\left(\frac{np}{d}\right)
holds as well. Noting that
\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\|^{2}=O\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)
for good
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
, and that
\mathbf{x}_{(1)}
is good w.p.
1-1/n^{2}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{1}\geqslant\Omega\left(\frac{np}{d}\right)

holds with probability
1-2/n^{2}
. Since this is true for each
\mathbf{y}_{i}
, we get from a union bound that it also holds for all
\mathbf{y}_{i}
w.h.p.
∎

9
